## Tahi response

## Investigating the Christchurch Mosque Attacks

The 15 March 2019 terrorist attack on Al Noor Mosque and Linwood Islamic Centre — in which a white supremacist gunman killed 51 worshippers and injured dozens more — prompted one of the most extensive official inquiries in New Zealand's history. [[1]](cite://1/1)

## The Royal Commission of Inquiry

A Royal Commission of Inquiry (known formally as the Royal Commission of Inquiry into the Terrorist Attack on Christchurch Masjidain) was established and released its report in December 2020. It made 44 recommendations covering a wide range of issues, including how the terrorist obtained his firearms and firearms licence, and failures within security and intelligence agencies. [[2]](cite://2/1)

The then-Labour government promised to implement all 44 recommendations. [[2]](cite://2/2)

## The Coronial Inquest

Separate from the Royal Commission, a coronial inquest — described as the largest and most complex in New Zealand's history — was established to examine the deaths of the 51 victims. [[3]](cite://3/1) It was conducted in two phases:

- **Phase one** dealt with the events of 15 March 2019 itself, examining what happened on the day and the emergency services response. [[3]](cite://3/2)
- **Phase two** examined the firearms licensing process that allowed the terrorist to legally obtain weapons, his online radicalisation, and the community's ability to respond to the risk of violent extremism. [[3]](cite://3/3)

The second phase inquest heard evidence about a police firearms licensing regime that was described as understaffed, underfunded and overworked prior to the attack. It also revealed a significant loophole that allowed people — including the terrorist — to obtain a standard firearms licence and purchase centrefire semi-automatic rifles fitted with high-capacity magazines, which were entirely unregulated at the time. [[4]](cite://4/1)

Critically, the inquest heard that police had made multiple attempts to warn politicians of successive governments about this danger, but that political will to address it was lacking until 51 lives were lost. [[4]](cite://4/2)

Many victims' families said they needed the coronial process to test and question evidence that the Royal Commission had only addressed in general terms — particularly around firearms licensing and the terrorist's online activity. [[3]](cite://3/4)

## Government Response to the Royal Commission

Of the 44 recommendations, 36 were implemented or integrated into ongoing work programmes. However, in August 2024, the coalition government announced it would not progress the remaining eight recommendations, declaring the coordinated response "concluded". [[2]](cite://2/3)

Changes that *were* implemented included:

- Creation of New Zealand's first National Security Strategy
- Publication of the first New Zealand Security Threat Environment Report
- Establishment of the Firearms Safety Authority and the Ministry for Ethnic Communities
- A public awareness campaign for identifying signs of violent extremism [[2]](cite://2/4)

The decision to shelve the final eight recommendations drew sharp criticism. Labour leader Chris Hipkins said "disappointing was not enough of a word," and accused the government of shirking its responsibility to the affected community. [[2]](cite://2/5) Members of the Muslim community, including Aliya Danzeisen of the Islamic Women's Council, noted that critical recommendations — including oversight of New Zealand's intelligence services — had been scrapped, leaving questions about how safe the country truly was. [[5]](cite://5/1)

## Ongoing Concerns

Six years on from the attack, as of March 2025, many community members remained deeply worried that a similar attack could happen again. Rosemary Omar, who lost her son Tariq in the massacre, said she had "very little faith that if the same thing was to happen tomorrow, the same mistakes would not be made." [[5]](cite://5/2)

There was also concern about the government's rewriting of the Arms Act, with one minister signalling she was open to liberalising access to the kinds of semi-automatic weapons used in the attack — something the Muslim community strongly opposed. [[6]](cite://6/1)

The coronial inquest's findings for both phases were still being written as of late 2024, with deputy chief coroner Brigitte Windley saying there remained significant complexities around balancing legitimate firearms use with public safety. [[4]](cite://4/3)

---

Would you like to explore what the eight scrapped Royal Commission recommendations actually were, and why the government chose not to progress them?

## Tahi response

## Water Services Reform in New Zealand: Where Things Stand

New Zealand's long-running water services debate has reached a significant milestone. The National-led coalition government's replacement framework for Labour's controversial Three Waters programme — branded **'Local Water Done Well'** — has now been fully legislated.

### The Final Chapter: Legislation Passed

The government's final piece of legislation to implement Local Water Done Well, the *Local Government (Water Services) Bill* and the *Local Government (Water Services) (Repeals and Amendments) Bill*, passed their third reading in August 2025. [[1]](cite://1/1) The bills provide what the government describes as a 'flexible' framework for local councils to address challenges related to drinking water, wastewater, and stormwater services. [[1]](cite://1/2)

NZ First's Casey Costello, who led the debate, said the legislation was the government's 'urgently required' plan to address challenges with New Zealand's water services, pointing to 'chronic underinvestment and the lack of sustainable management' as the core problems. [[1]](cite://1/3)

### How We Got Here

The road to this point has been turbulent:

- Labour's original Three Waters proposal (later rebranded 'Affordable Water Reform') would have shifted drinking water, wastewater, and stormwater management from local councils into large, independent entities — starting with four, then expanding to 10 — capable of borrowing at far greater scale. [[2]](cite://2/1)
- Fierce opposition centred on concerns about asset ownership leaving local hands, and the co-governance model that would have given mana whenua (tribal authority holders) a 50 percent presence on strategic oversight boards. [[2]](cite://2/2)
- When the current coalition government took office, it repealed the Three Waters legislation under urgency in February 2024 — the Water Services Acts Repeal Bill passing with the support of National, ACT, and NZ First. [[2]](cite://2/3)[[3]](cite://3/1)
- An estimated $1.2 billion had been spent setting up the Three Waters system before it was scrapped. [[4]](cite://4/1)

### What Local Water Done Well Actually Does

Rather than large centralised entities, the new framework allows councils to voluntarily form council controlled organisations (CCOs) — bodies similar to Wellington Water and Auckland's Watercare — that can band together regionally and achieve balance sheet separation from debt-laden councils, theoretically enabling cheaper borrowing. [[4]](cite://4/2)[[2]](cite://2/4)

Crucially, the government has confirmed it will **not** underwrite council borrowing or provide financial assistance in setting up CCOs — it sees water as a local government responsibility. [[5]](cite://5/1)

### The Debate Isn't Over

The legislation passed largely on coalition votes, with strong opposition criticism:

- **Labour's Megan Woods** argued the changes 'shift the financial risk' to councils and ratepayers, and said scrapping Three Waters eliminated economies of scale that could have saved money. [[1]](cite://1/4)
- **Te Pāti Māori's Mariameno Kapa Kingi** called the bill 'an atrocious piece of work', criticising the removal of provisions recognising Māori roles in water services delivery and saying it ignored obligations under Te Tiriti o Waitangi. [[1]](cite://1/5)
- Some councils, particularly smaller and more indebted ones, have warned of significant rates increases ahead. Rangitīkei mayor Andy Watson described the situation as 'a ticking time bomb', warning some councils could be 'just about insolvent'. [[6]](cite://6/1)
- A report from late 2025 indicated council plans under Local Water Done Well are expected to cost $9 billion more than anticipated. [[7]](cite://7/1)

### The Underlying Challenge Remains

Whatever the political colour of the solution, the core problem is unchanged: New Zealand's water infrastructure — much of it a century old — requires enormous investment. Estimates of up to $185 billion over 30 years have been cited, and the question of who pays — ratepayers, councils, or central government — remains deeply contested. [[4]](cite://4/3)[[6]](cite://6/2)

Would you like to explore how specific regions or councils are responding to the new Local Water Done Well framework?

## Tahi response

# Flooding in Nelson and Marlborough

The Nelson, Tasman and Marlborough regions of Te Tauihu (the top of the South Island) have a long and significant history with flooding — from 19th-century deluges that floated churches downriver, to a string of devastating events in 2025 that left communities reeling.

![embed-1](embed://1)

## A Region Built on Flood-Prone Land

The geography of the region makes it inherently vulnerable. The alluvial valleys of the Wairau and Awatere rivers are among Marlborough's most recently formed landscapes, and the Wairau Valley was largely swamp until flood control measures were put in place. [[1]](cite://1/1) Intense rainfall over mountain ranges, combined with rivers running swiftly to the sea, means flooding can strike quickly and with devastating force.

## Historical Floods

**Blenheim, 1868**
One of the most dramatic early accounts comes from 3 February 1868, when severe flooding left much of Blenheim submerged. The *Marlborough Express* described the scene vividly: a partly built Presbyterian church floated off its piles and crashed into the Ōmaka River bridge, while livestock, furniture and fencing were swept downstream. [[2]](cite://2/1)

**Lower North Island and Marlborough, February 2004**
Between 14 and 17 February 2004, intense rain — up to 300 millimetres in two days — fell on already saturated land. The Waitohi River flooded parts of Picton, and 500 people were evacuated amid fears a water supply dam might collapse. [[3]](cite://3/1) The February floods cost over $112 million in insurance payouts alone, with the government providing $135 million in aid to farmers — around 2,600 of whom were affected. [[3]](cite://3/2)[[4]](cite://4/1)

**Northwest Nelson — A 150-Year Flood**
Near the end of December (year unspecified in the source), the Aorere River in northwest Nelson experienced its worst flood in 150 years after more than 200 millimetres of rain fell across a large area, with one rain gauge in the mountains recording nearly 400 millimetres. Bridges were destroyed, farmland inundated, and the river carved a completely new path to the sea. [[5]](cite://5/1)

**Nelson and Marlborough Flash Floods**
On one occasion, a line of thunderstorms brought rainfall of between 40 and 55 millimetres in a single hour to Nelson and Woodbourne airports, as well as the Richmond Ranges, causing widespread flash flooding and water entering homes and shops in both Blenheim and Nelson. The East Branch of the Motueka River rose almost five metres, washing away a hut where two Department of Conservation hunters were staying — one was found deceased four kilometres downstream, the other presumed dead. [[6]](cite://6/1)

**Nelson and Tasman, December 2011**
Starting 13 December 2011, a stalled weather trough triggered what became one of the most tumultuous summers in the region's history. Unusually, the heaviest rainfall landed close to the coast — only two to five kilometres from the sea — rather than in the inland catchments. Hillsides collapsed, rivers burst their banks, and torrents of silt and sand swept through valleys across the district. [[7]](cite://7/1)[[8]](cite://8/1)

## The Devastating Events of 2025

The year 2025 brought an extraordinary sequence of flood events to the region, described by Civil Defence as the worst flooding in almost 150 years. [[9]](cite://9/1)

**May 2025 — Nelson Flash Floods**
On 26 May 2025, Nelson was caught off guard by flash flooding, with around 40 millimetres of rain falling since midnight and nearly 20 millimetres between 9am and 11am. The suburb of Toi Toi was among the worst affected. [[10]](cite://10/1) Manhole covers were blown out of their holes, roads were blocked, and water spilled into shops. Fire and Emergency New Zealand (FENZ) responded to more than 20 incidents, with appliances brought in from as far as Stoke and Hira. [[11]](cite://11/1) Cows were seen being swept down swollen rivers — at least one died. [[11]](cite://11/2) Notably, the region was not under any MetService rain warning at the time. [[11]](cite://11/3)

**June 2025 — State of Emergency Declared**
On 26 June 2025, a severe weather event caused the Motueka River to burst its banks and triggered widespread flooding across Nelson and Tasman. [[12]](cite://12/1) A state of emergency was declared for the Nelson Tasman region on 11 July 2025, with Civil Defence issuing emergency mobile alerts urging people to get to higher ground due to the risk to life from flooding, slips and high rivers. [[13]](cite://13/1)

In Marlborough, Northbank Road was blocked by a massive slip involving rocks the size of vans, cutting off access to at least 100 properties along the Wairau River's north bank. [[14]](cite://14/1) The Spring Creek stopbank — already weakened by the 2016 Kaikōura earthquake and further damaged by flooding in July 2021 — came under serious threat, and residents on Dodson, Hathaway, March and part of Ferry Road were ordered to evacuate. [[15]](cite://15/1)

**July 2025 — A Second Devastating Storm**
Just weeks after the first major event, a second severe storm struck the already battered region in mid-July 2025. Dozens were placed in emergency accommodation, multiple schools closed, and Nelson Tasman Civil Defence confirmed this was the worst flooding in the region in almost 150 years. [[9]](cite://9/2)[[16]](cite://16/1) Nelson Tasman group controller Alec Louverdis described the damage to property and infrastructure in Tasman as "substantial" and said recovery would take time. [[12]](cite://12/2)

**State of Emergency Lifted, 15 July 2025**
The state of emergency was lifted on 17 July 2025, with the region entering a one-month transition period. Tasman mayor Tim King acknowledged his community had "faced two major weather events" in three weeks, with "significant impacts across the region." [[17]](cite://17/1) Nelson mayor Nick Smith noted the main ongoing impact in Nelson was the closure of State Highway 6 Rocks Road, but expressed hope that Nelson would be "back to normal and fully open for business" by the weekend. [[17]](cite://17/2)

## Infrastructure and Future Preparedness

The Spring Creek stopbank in Marlborough has been a particular focus of concern. An $8.7 million repair programme was agreed, including $3.6 million in government funding, with construction set to begin in November 2026. [[15]](cite://15/2) In the aftermath of the 2025 floods, calls were also made for improved rain radar coverage in Nelson, given the region's increasing vulnerability. [[18]](cite://18/1)

Would you like to know more about the infrastructure being built to protect these communities from future flooding?

## Tahi response

## The Resource Management Act: Aotearoa's Planning Backbone

The Resource Management Act (RMA) 1991 is New Zealand's principal piece of environmental and planning legislation. Hailed at the time as the first comprehensive law in the world to require sustainable management of resources, it replaced roughly 80 earlier acts and orders in one sweeping reform [[1]](cite://1/1). Its architect was Sir Geoffrey Palmer, who described it as 'one of the most comprehensive reforms in the Western world' [[2]](cite://2/1).

At its core, the RMA requires all developments — with the exception of mineral extraction, which is covered separately by the Crown Minerals Act 1991 — to avoid, mitigate, or remedy adverse environmental effects [[1]](cite://1/2). Resource consents for the use of water, air, soil, and land must be obtained from the relevant regional, city, or district councils, with provision for public participation in decision-making [[1]](cite://1/3).

The act also replaced the old Town and Country Planning Act and required councils to prepare new district plans to manage land use in an environmentally sustainable way. An Environment Court replaced the Planning Tribunal [[3]](cite://3/1). Its philosophical ethos, at least initially, was 'more free market than regulatory' — as long as there was no environmental harm, people were largely free to build as they wished [[3]](cite://3/2).

### What the RMA Covers

The RMA's reach is broad. It controls land use, manages water quality, regulates air emissions, and protects coastal areas and estuaries [[4]](cite://4/1)[[5]](cite://5/1)[[6]](cite://6/1)[[7]](cite://7/1). Regional councils are responsible for monitoring these areas and issuing permits and consents accordingly [[8]](cite://8/1)[[5]](cite://5/2).

Notably, the 1991 act was a landmark moment for Māori. Prior legislation had largely ignored Māori historical, spiritual, and cultural concerns. The RMA explicitly recognised Māori spiritual and cultural values and the principles of the Treaty of Waitangi. Environmental developments required consultation with iwi (tribes), and resource consent applications had to be sent to iwi authorities [[9]](cite://9/1).

However, not everything falls under the RMA's umbrella. Biosecurity — including border protection for agriculture and forestry — is governed separately under the Biosecurity Act 1993 [[10]](cite://10/1).

### The Case for Reform

Despite its ambitions, the RMA has been criticised from multiple directions over the years. Palmer himself acknowledged it wasn't perfect: 'You can have development, or you can have sustainability. There's an inherent contradiction in this which we haven't resolved yet' [[11]](cite://11/1).

Environmental advocates have argued that successive governments failed to provide adequate policy guidance to regional councils, and that councils themselves had increasingly become 'development agencies on behalf of government', undermining the act's protective intent [[12]](cite://12/1). Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment Jan Wright warned that proposed changes amounted to a 'Trojan horse', arguing the RMA itself provides the balance to economic imperatives and 'is not, and should not become, an economic development act' [[13]](cite://13/1).

On the other side, critics have long complained the RMA makes it too hard and too costly to build housing, infrastructure, and renewable energy. Federated Farmers called the current system 'broken', with farmers spending excessive time and money on resource consents for straightforward activities [[14]](cite://14/1).

There has been bipartisan recognition that the act has both failed to protect the environment sufficiently and wrapped projects in excessive red tape [[15]](cite://15/1).

## The Coalition Government's Reform Programme

The current National-led coalition government has pursued the most ambitious overhaul of the RMA in its history, structured in three phases.

**Phase one** involved repealing legislation introduced by the previous Labour government and making urgent early amendments, including revisions to stock exclusion rules, winter grazing, Te Mana o te Wai (the concept of water as a taonga — a treasured resource), mining consenting, and the suspension of Significant Natural Area (SNA) requirements [[16]](cite://16/1).

**Phase two** introduced a second RMA Amendment Bill focused on infrastructure, energy, housing, farming, and natural hazards, alongside the single largest package of national direction changes in New Zealand's history — seven new national direction instruments and amendments to 14 existing ones [[17]](cite://17/1). A Fast-track Approvals Bill was also introduced as a 'one-stop shop' consenting regime for significant projects [[18]](cite://18/1).

**Phase three** — the most sweeping — proposes to scrap the RMA entirely and replace it with two new laws [[19]](cite://19/1)[[15]](cite://15/2):

- A **Natural Environment Act**, focused on managing environmental effects from activities, including setting quantified environmental limits at a regional level [[15]](cite://15/3)
- A **Planning Act**, focused on enabling urban development and infrastructure [[19]](cite://19/2)

RMA Reform minister Chris Bishop said the goal is to cut administrative and compliance costs by 45 percent, grounded in the principle of 'the enjoyment of property rights' — allowing people to do more on their own land as long as it doesn't harm others [[20]](cite://20/1). The reforms also propose more standardised zoning nationally (New Zealand currently has 1,175 different zone types across its councils, compared to just 13 in Japan) and a new planning disputes tribunal for faster, lower-cost resolution of disagreements [[20]](cite://20/2)[[15]](cite://15/4).

Notably, the reforms would remove the Treaty of Waitangi clause currently in the RMA [[20]](cite://20/3).

In December 2025, the coalition formally introduced the Planning Bill and Natural Environment Bill to Parliament, with the aim of passing them before the 2026 election [[21]](cite://21/1). Labour leader Chris Hipkins indicated his party would not repeal the legislation if elected, though it would likely make amendments — suggesting the 'repeal and replace cycle' that has plagued RMA reform for decades may finally be drawing to a close [[21]](cite://21/2).

### Reactions

Responses to the reforms have been mixed. Federated Farmers welcomed the shift, saying the RMA had been 'the single biggest handbrake on growing agricultural productivity' [[21]](cite://21/3). Local Government New Zealand expressed readiness to adapt, though its representatives acknowledged a 'tsunami of change' ahead for councils [[21]](cite://21/4). Environmental groups such as Forest & Bird have raised concerns about the pace of reform and potential erosion of environmental protections, particularly around freshwater [[22]](cite://22/1)[[15]](cite://15/5).

Would you like to explore a specific aspect of the reforms in more detail — for example, the freshwater protections, or how the Fast-track Approvals regime works?

## Tahi response

## Hauora Māori — Health Outcomes for Māori in Aotearoa

The health gap between Māori and non-Māori in New Zealand is one of the country's most persistent and well-documented inequities. While there have been meaningful improvements over time, Māori continue to experience poorer health outcomes across almost every measure compared to non-Māori.

![embed-1](embed://1)

## Life Expectancy

The most striking headline statistic is the gap in life expectancy. According to the most recent Stats NZ data (2022–2024), Māori life expectancy at birth is 75.8 years — the lowest of any ethnic group in New Zealand [[1]](cite://1/1). By comparison, people who identify as Pākehā or other ethnicities have a life expectancy of 82.8 years, a gap of roughly seven years [[1]](cite://1/2)[[2]](cite://2/1).

There is a positive note here, however: Māori life expectancy has increased by 3.1 years from the 2005–2007 period to 2022–2024, a larger increase than any other ethnic group over the same timeframe [[1]](cite://1/3). Earlier data from 2012–2014 recorded life expectancy at birth as 77 years for Māori women and 73 years for Māori men, compared to 84 years for non-Māori women and 80 years for non-Māori men [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1).

On average, Māori die younger than non-Māori — a pattern consistently recorded in national data [[5]](cite://5/1).

## Chronic Disease

The disparities extend across a wide range of serious conditions:

**Heart disease** is one of the starkest examples. A 2025 report by Otago University, commissioned by the Kia Manawanui Trust, found that Māori and Pasifika (Pacific peoples) die from heart disease on average ten years younger than Pākehā [[6]](cite://6/1)[[7]](cite://7/1). Māori also have the highest rate of death from heart disease of any ethnic group in New Zealand [[8]](cite://8/1). Data from the 2010s showed Māori were dying from heart disease at more than twice the rate of non-Māori [[9]](cite://9/1).

**Cancer** figures are similarly concerning. In the 2010s, Māori men were almost three times as likely as non-Māori men to die of lung cancer; Māori women were over four times as likely as non-Māori women [[9]](cite://9/2).

**Diabetes** has reached near-epidemic levels in some Māori communities. Māori were twice as likely as non-Māori to have diabetes, with higher complication rates as well [[9]](cite://9/3). In the 2000s, it was estimated that well over half of those with diabetes in some Māori communities didn't even know they had it [[10]](cite://10/1).

## Child and Youth Health

Māori and Pasifika children are disproportionately affected by respiratory conditions, rheumatic fever and heart disease, skin infections, dental disease, and mental health problems [[11]](cite://11/1). Hospitalisation rates for respiratory conditions among Māori and Pasifika children have been largely attributed to disparities in access to warm, dry housing — a direct consequence of income inequality [[12]](cite://12/1).

While there have been some improvements — meningococcal admissions and skin infection rates among young Māori declined between 2010 and 2014 — the rates of skin infections among Māori aged 0–24 remained twice as high as those for non-Māori youth even after that decline [[12]](cite://12/2).

## Mental Health

The Mental Health and Wellbeing Commission's latest reporting (June 2026) paints a troubling picture. Some 22.5 percent of Māori experienced "high" or "very high" psychological distress in the four weeks prior to being surveyed — well above the 14 percent national average [[13]](cite://13/1). The age-standardised rate of suicide deaths is highest among Māori men aged 25 to 44 years [[14]](cite://14/1)[[15]](cite://15/1).

Despite this elevated need, Māori are not being adequately prioritised for mental health support. Fewer people can access kaupapa Māori (Māori-centred) primary mental health services, funding for those services has not kept pace with broader mental health investment, and only 29 percent of Māori accessing specialist services use kaupapa Māori services [[14]](cite://14/2)[[15]](cite://15/2).

In inpatient settings, Māori in adult services were 6.6 times more likely to be placed in seclusion (solitary confinement) than non-Māori — a practice the commission describes as often traumatising [[15]](cite://15/3).

## Access to Care

Access to healthcare remains a significant barrier. Factors including cost, availability of doctors, geographic isolation, and the attitudes of medical personnel all contribute to poorer health outcomes for Māori [[10]](cite://10/2). Māori living in rural areas face compounding disadvantages — Māori under 30 living remotely were twice as likely to die from a preventable disease as their urban peers [[16]](cite://16/1).

Research also shows that Māori with mental health conditions were 7.7 percent more likely than non-Māori to report difficulties securing a GP appointment, with experiences of feeling less heard and respected by healthcare providers [[17]](cite://17/1).

## A Long-Standing Inequity

These disparities are not new. Health differences between Māori and Pākehā have been documented since the 19th century, and despite significant improvements — particularly in the decades following World War II — the gap has proven stubbornly persistent [[18]](cite://18/1)[[19]](cite://19/1). As researchers note, despite great improvements and a significant rise in life expectancy, Māori are still worse affected than non-Māori by almost every known health condition [[9]](cite://9/4).

The causes are understood to be deeply structural — rooted in poverty, housing quality, access to healthcare, and the legacy of historical inequities [[10]](cite://10/3)[[11]](cite://11/2).

Would you like to explore what specific policies or initiatives have been introduced to address these health disparities for Māori?

## Tahi response

## Te Kāhui Tupua: Taranaki Maunga becomes a legal person

On 30 January 2025, a landmark moment in New Zealand's Treaty of Waitangi history unfolded at Parliament when the Taranaki Maunga Collective Redress Bill passed its second and third readings, granting legal personhood to Taranaki Maunga. Around 400 people from the eight iwi of Taranaki were present to witness the occasion. [[1]](cite://1/1)

## What the Settlement Entails

Legal personhood is not bestowed on the central peak alone. Under the name **Te Kāhui Tupua**, it extends to "a living and indivisible whole comprising Taranaki Maunga and other tūpuna maunga (ancestral mountains), including Pouākai and Kaitake, from their peaks to, and including, all the surrounding lands, and incorporating all their physical and metaphysical elements." [[2]](cite://2/1)

In practical terms, this means:

- Te Kāhui Tupua can be defended in court and can own property [[2]](cite://2/2)
- Ownership of Te Papa Kura o Taranaki — formerly Egmont National Park — has been vested in it, with the ability to add to that holding [[2]](cite://2/3)
- The park is managed by **Te Tōpuni Kōkōrangi**, a trust board nominated by iwi and the Minister of Conservation, working collaboratively under a co-governance model [[1]](cite://1/2)[[2]](cite://2/4)
- The name 'Mt Egmont' ceases to exist as an official geographic name; the park is now Te Papa Kura o Taranaki, meaning 'the highly regarded and treasured lands of Taranaki', and the highest peak is officially Taranaki Maunga [[1]](cite://1/3)

## The Crown's Apology

Central to the settlement was a profound Crown apology for its confiscation of Taranaki Maunga and almost half a million hectares (1.2 million acres) of Taranaki lands in 1865 — described as a fundamental breach of Te Tiriti o Waitangi. [[1]](cite://1/4)

The Crown's formal acknowledgements in the bill were sweeping. It acknowledged that:

- The confiscation of Taranaki Maunga was wrongful and unjust and caused "immeasurable distress" to the people of Taranaki [[2]](cite://2/5)
- It had misled the West Coast Commission in 1880, falsely stating the maunga had already been made a forest reserve, which effectively excluded it from being returned to Māori [[2]](cite://2/6)
- When Egmont National Park was established, the Crown failed to consult iwi or consider their customary interests, preventing them from exercising kaitiakitanga (guardianship) [[2]](cite://2/7)
- It failed for many years to provide Māori representation in the park's governance [[2]](cite://2/8)

## Why It Matters

Lead negotiator Jamie Tuuta explained the deep cultural logic behind legal personhood: "Our worldview is our maunga are ancestors, they're not resources but they are living beings and so the notion of legal personhood fits well with our worldview." [[1]](cite://1/5)

Iwi negotiator Liana Poutu (Te Ātiawa) noted it was the end of a very long journey — one begun by generations of advocates who never lived to see its completion. "Over 200 petitions were sent to Parliament for this exact same thing," she said, adding that her own children "will never know that Taranaki's name was ever Egmont." [[1]](cite://1/6)

## The Eight Taranaki Iwi

The settlement serves as collective redress for all eight iwi of Taranaki: Ngaa Rauru Kiitahi, Ngāruahine, Ngāti Maru, Ngāti Mutunga, Ngāti Ruanui, Ngāti Tama, Taranaki iwi and Te Ātiawa. [[1]](cite://1/7)

## New Zealand Precedent

This is the third time in New Zealand that a geographical feature has been granted legal personhood — following Te Awa Tupua (Whanganui River) in 2017 and Te Urewera National Park in 2014. [[3]](cite://3/1)[[2]](cite://2/9) University of Otago researcher Viktoria Kahui noted that such recognition "shifts the whole mindset" around a natural feature — giving it a voice, representation in decision-making, and explicit acknowledgement of the connection between tangata whenua and their environment. [[4]](cite://4/1)

Would you like to know more about the long history of Māori efforts to reclaim Taranaki Maunga — from the 1860s confiscations through to the negotiations that finally led to this settlement?

## Sources

[1] [Taranaki Maunga Becomes a Legal Person as Treaty Settlement Passes into Law](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/national/540431/taranaki-maunga-becomes-a-legal-person-as-treaty-settlement-passes-into-law)
[2] [The Maunga Owns Itself: Taranaki Histories, Crown Apology, and Treaty Settlement](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/thehouse/540549/the-maunga-owns-itself-taranaki-histories-and-apologies)
[3] [Blunder on Mt Zorro: The Politics of a Hawke's Bay Landmark](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/blunder-on-mt-zorro/)

## Official Cash Rate: 3 percent

The most recent search result I have on this topic is from 20 August 2025, which reports that the Reserve Bank of New Zealand (RBNZ) cut its Official Cash Rate (OCR) by 25 basis points to **3 percent** — a three-year low at the time of that decision. [[1]](cite://1/1)

The Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) voted four to two in favour of the smaller 25 basis point cut, having debated options of holding, cutting by 25 basis points, or cutting by 50 basis points. [[1]](cite://1/2)

### What the Reserve Bank said at the time

The RBNZ left the door open for further cuts, noting: *"If medium term inflation pressures continue to ease as expected, there is scope to lower the OCR further."* The bank projected the OCR could trough at around 2.5 percent by the end of 2025, suggesting further cuts were likely through the course of that year. [[1]](cite://1/3)

### Important caveat

Today is 17 June 2026, and my most recent search result on this topic is dated 20 August 2025. It's quite possible the OCR has changed since then — the RBNZ reviews the rate roughly every six weeks [[2]](cite://2/1), so there could have been several decisions since that report. For the most up-to-date figure, I'd recommend checking directly at **rbnz.govt.nz**.

## Tahi response

## Te Whāea o te Motu — Dame Whina Cooper and the 1975 Land March

Dame Whina Cooper was the central figure of the 1975 hīkoi (land march) — its leader, its inspiration, and its most powerful symbol. At around 80 years of age, she led Te Rōpū o te Matakite (the group of visionaries, or seers) on a march of roughly 1,000 kilometres from Te Hāpua in the Far North all the way to Parliament in Wellington. [[1]](cite://1/1)

![embed-1](embed://1)

## How it Began

In early 1975, a hui (gathering) about land alienation was held at Te Puea marae in Māngere Bridge, Auckland. Cooper attended and spoke passionately about the consequences of inaction. Several groups united under the banner of Te Rōpū o te Matakite and asked Cooper to lead them. She accepted — and proposed a march. [[2]](cite://2/1)

Cooper was already a figure of enormous mana (prestige and authority), having served as the inaugural president of the Māori Women's Welfare League in 1951. [[3]](cite://3/1) Her energy, reputation and whakapapa (genealogy) brought together a remarkable coalition — from official organisations like the New Zealand Māori Council and the Māori Women's Welfare League, to more outspoken activists such as members of Ngā Tamatoa. [[2]](cite://2/2)

## Setting the Tone

Cooper was determined the march would be above reproach. She banned alcohol and insisted no placards or signs be carried. The sacred hīkoi would be heralded only by a pouwhenua (a carved pole representing land occupation) and a single white flag representing te Matakite, which was never to touch the ground. [[2]](cite://2/3)

On Māori Language Day, 14 September, around 50 whānau (family) departed from Te Hāpua. Cooper took those now-iconic first steps, holding the hand of her mokopuna (grandchild) Irenee. [[2]](cite://2/4)

![embed-2](embed://2)

## Leading the March South

As the march moved south, stopping at marae along the route, numbers swelled significantly — reaching around 5,000 by the time the marchers arrived in Wellington on 13 October 1975. [[4]](cite://4/1) Cooper's instinct for the dramatic and symbolic kept media attention high throughout. [[5]](cite://5/1)

At Parliament, Cooper presented Prime Minister Bill Rowling with a Memorial of Right — signed by 200 Māori elders — and a petition backed by 60,000 signatures demanding that no further Māori land be alienated. [[4]](cite://4/2)

## Legacy and Impact

The march brought Māori concerns into the mainstream and helped politicise a generation of Māori. [[2]](cite://2/5) In the words of one source, it "ignited an irreversible change in the relationship between Māori and the Crown." [[2]](cite://2/6) Later that same year, the Treaty of Waitangi Act established the Waitangi Tribunal to hear contemporary grievances against the Crown. [[3]](cite://3/2)

Cooper's contribution to the cause earned her the enduring title *Te Whāea o te Motu* — Mother of the Nation. [[6]](cite://6/1) She was made a Dame Commander of the Order of the British Empire (DBE) in 1981. [[4]](cite://4/3)

Would you like to explore what happened in the aftermath of the march — including the Memorial of Right and the Waitangi Tribunal's establishment that same year?

## Sources

[2] [Inside the Land March: Te Rōpū o te Matakite and Māori Activism](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/inside-the-land-march/)
[3] [Māori Organisations: Evolution, Activism, and Cultural Development in New Zealand](https://teara.govt.nz/en/nga-ropu-maori-organisations)
[4] [Whina Cooper: A Māori Leader's Life, Legacy, and Activism](https://teara.govt.nz/en/biographies/5c32/cooper-whina)
[5] [Inside the Land March: Te Rōpū o te Matakite and Māori Activism](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/inside-the-land-march/)

## Tahi response

## The Waitangi Tribunal's Findings on the Ngāpuhi Claim

The Waitangi Tribunal's landmark 2014 report on the Ngāpuhi claim — known as the Te Raki inquiry (Wai 1040) — reached a significant and historic conclusion: that Ngāpuhi rangatira (chiefs) **did not cede their sovereignty** when they signed Te Tiriti o Waitangi in February 1840. [[1]](cite://1/1)

## The Core Finding

In the Tribunal's own words, as recorded in its report: the rangatira who signed Te Tiriti did not cede their authority to make and enforce law over their people or their territories. Rather, they agreed to *share* power and authority with the Governor — agreeing to a relationship, not a surrender. [[2]](cite://2/1)

In short, the Tribunal found that Te Tiriti was an agreement to enter into a partnership, not an act of submission to British sovereignty. [[3]](cite://3/1)

## What This Means

The finding rests heavily on the difference between the English and te reo Māori versions of the Treaty. The English text speaks of sovereignty passing to the British Crown, while the Māori text — which experts say takes legal precedence — speaks of *tino rangatiratanga* (self-determination) being guaranteed *to* Māori. As academic Carwyn Jones has noted, tino rangatiratanga is much closer in meaning to sovereignty than to mere chieftainship, making it inconsistent with any reading that Māori ceded sovereignty in Te Tiriti. [[4]](cite://4/1)[[5]](cite://5/1)

## The Stage Two Report

The Tribunal's work on the Ngāpuhi claim did not end in 2014. A Stage Two report — more than 10 years in the making, based on 26 weeks of hearings and more than 500,000 pages of evidence relating to 415 individual Treaty claims — was handed over to Ngāpuhi in December 2023. [[6]](cite://6/1)

Claimants have subsequently asked the Tribunal to reiterate the finding that Te Tiriti o Waitangi is an international treaty between two sovereign states, and that Māori did not cede their sovereignty. [[7]](cite://7/1)

## Ongoing Significance

The findings continue to shape debates around Treaty settlements and constitutional arrangements today. Ngāpuhi hapū have made sovereignty a firm "red line" in any future settlement negotiations with the Crown, with Te Kotahitanga o Ngā Hapū Ngāpuhi co-chair Pita Tipene stating he would not enter settlement discussions without acknowledgement of hapū sovereignty. [[1]](cite://1/2)

Would you like to explore how the Crown has responded to these findings, and where Ngāpuhi's Treaty settlement negotiations currently stand?

[2] [Looking for Relationship: Sovereignty, TPPA, and Treaty Negotiations in New Zealand](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/looking-for-relationship/)
[3] [The Stones Are Talking: The Waitangi Tribunal's Findings on Maori Sovereignty](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/the-stones-are-talking/)

[5] [Sovereignty Debate Sees Major Parties Weighing In for First Time](https://www.rnz.co.nz/national/programmes/focusonpolitics/audio/2018953469/sovereignty-debate-sees-major-parties-weighing-in-for-first-time)

## Tahi response

## The Climate Change Response (Zero Carbon) Amendment Act 2019

New Zealand passed the Climate Change Response (Zero Carbon) Amendment Act in 2019, which amended the existing Climate Change Response Act 2002 and transformed it from a policy tool into a legislative backbone for emissions reduction. [[1]](cite://1/1) Described at the time as a world first — a law with cross-party support that would enshrine climate ambition in legislation — it represented a significant step in Aotearoa's commitment to the Paris Agreement. [[2]](cite://2/1)[[1]](cite://1/2)

Here are the Act's core provisions:

### Legally Binding Long-Term Emissions Targets

The Act set legally binding targets intended to align with global efforts to limit warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels. Specifically, it committed Aotearoa to: [[1]](cite://1/3)

- Reaching **net zero greenhouse gas emissions** (excluding biogenic methane) by 2050
- Cutting **biogenic methane** from livestock and waste by 24–47 percent by 2050
- An **interim methane reduction target** of 10 percent by 2030

### A System of Five-Yearly Emissions Budgets

The Act introduced a system of five-yearly emissions budgets that cap total emissions across the economy. These budgets break down the distant 2050 target into a series of closer, smaller and more manageable goals, and are not sector-specific. [[3]](cite://3/1)

### Emissions Reduction Plans

For each budget period, the government is required to produce an Emissions Reduction Plan (ERP) — setting out the policy actions across different sectors (energy, transport, agriculture, industry) that, taken together, should produce a viable path to the necessary emissions reductions. [[3]](cite://3/2)

### An Independent Climate Change Commission

The Climate Change Commission was established as an independent body to provide transparency and expert judgement on targets, budgets, and plans. Its advice is made public, and the government is generally required to respond publicly — helping ensure climate policy is "open, well-informed, systematic, effective and equitable." [[3]](cite://3/3)

### Paris Agreement Alignment

The Act was designed to anchor New Zealand's climate action to its international commitments under the Paris Agreement, with ETS (Emissions Trading Scheme) settings required to accord with what New Zealand had promised internationally. [[4]](cite://4/1)

### A Quasi-Constitutional Framework

Legal experts Barry Barton and Jennifer Campion of the University of Waikato have described the Act's zero carbon provisions as having "a quasi-constitutional character" — like the Public Finance Act or the Electoral Act — providing the rules and structure within which New Zealand makes climate decisions. [[3]](cite://3/4)

### The Act Under Pressure

It's worth noting that since the coalition government came to power in 2023, many of the policies designed to meet the Act's targets have been repealed or defunded. The Climate Change Commission's 2025 monitoring report warned the government's current plan "won't be enough" to meet the second and third emissions budgets covering the decade to 2035. [[1]](cite://1/4) The government has also proposed further changes to the Act, including removing the requirement for the Commission to advise on emissions reduction plans and reducing public consultation on national emissions budgets. [[4]](cite://4/2)

Would you like to explore what specific policies have been scrapped or retained since 2023, and what that means for New Zealand's chances of meeting its 2050 targets?

## Sources

[1] [New Zealand's Zero Carbon Act: From Groundbreaking Climate Law to Policy Shell](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/political/570336/how-jacinda-ardern-s-groundbreaking-climate-law-has-become-a-shell)
[2] [Climate Change: Global Systems, New Zealand Impacts, and Future Projections](https://teara.govt.nz/en/climate-change)
[3] [The Government's Dismantling of Climate Laws Breaks Years of Cross-Party Agreement](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/political/578170/the-government-s-dismantling-of-climate-laws-breaks-years-of-cross-party-agreement)

## Tahi response

## The Whakaari/White Island Coronial Inquest: Where Things Stand

It's important to note that, based on the available search results, the coroner — Marcus Elliott — has **not yet issued final conclusions or findings**. The inquest is still a work in progress, with phase one having wrapped up in late November 2025 and a second phase planned for 2026.

Here's what the search results tell us about where the process is at:

### Phase One: Emergency Response (October–November 2025)

The first phase of the coronial inquest began in Whakatāne on 3 October 2025 — nearly six years after the 9 December 2019 eruption, which killed 22 people and seriously injured 25 others [[1]](cite://1/1). It focused on the events of the day itself and the emergency and medical response, including the timing, location, and cause of each death [[1]](cite://1/2).

When phase one concluded in late November 2025, counsel for bereaved families and survivors, Anna Adams, described the process as 'valuable' but said significant questions remained unanswered [[2]](cite://2/1).

### Key Issues Examined

Several serious concerns were aired during phase one:

- **Civilian-only rescue:** All 39 people rescued from the island on the day were saved entirely by civilian boats and helicopters, with no official emergency services landing on Whakaari for several hours after the eruption [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1).
- **Communications failures:** An InterCAD system failure caused an approximately seven-minute delay in notifying St John Ambulance of the eruption. A further 13-minute delay occurred between police receiving the first 111 call and transferring it to InterCAD [[2]](cite://2/2).
- **No police radio at the Emergency Operations Centre (EOC):** The EOC in Whakatāne was described as a communications 'black spot', with poor wifi connectivity and phone signal [[4]](cite://4/2).
- **Confusion over lead agency:** There was acknowledged confusion about whether Civil Defence or police was the lead agency, though counsel said this confusion did not appear to have impacted the response [[2]](cite://2/3).
- **The eruption plan wasn't used:** Police had access to a Whakaari eruption response plan before the disaster, but it was not used as a focal point of the emergency response [[4]](cite://4/3).
- **Decision to stand down rescuers:** The Major Operations Centre in Wellington ordered that no one physically set foot on the island just before 5.00pm that day, after GNS advised the volcano could erupt again. Eight people were left on the island; their bodies were not retrieved for a week, and two were never recovered [[4]](cite://4/4)[[5]](cite://5/1).

### Recommendations Signalled

While the coroner has yet to issue formal findings, several recommendations were submitted by counsel for various agencies:

- That a single, unified multi-agency emergency response plan be developed, rather than multiple separate plans [[2]](cite://2/4).
- That the way scientific and technical advice is communicated to emergency services in connection with high-risk volcanic activities be reviewed [[2]](cite://2/5).
- Many of the identified communication failures have already been fixed and updated since the eruption [[2]](cite://2/6).

### What's Still to Come

The country did not seem ready for the disaster, the inquest heard, despite Whakaari being New Zealand's most active volcano [[3]](cite://3/2). Phase two of the inquest — due to begin in 2026 — will explore the period leading up to the eruption, including regulatory oversight of tours to Whakaari, risk management, and whether visitors had adequate information about the risks they were taking [[2]](cite://2/7)[[6]](cite://6/1).

As Adams put it: *'Many of the families and survivors continue to hold the view that they should not have been present walking in the crater of the Whakaari Island volcano that day'* [[2]](cite://2/8).

Would you like to know more about what the bereaved families and survivors have said about what they hope comes out of phase two of the inquest?

## Sources

[2] [Whakaari Inquest Phase One Concludes: Bereaved Families Seek Answers on Safety and Emergency Response](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/national/580327/findings-from-first-phase-of-whakaari-inquest-valuable-but-questions-remain-for-bereaved-families)
[3] [Coronial Inquiry into Whakaari Eruption: Country Was Not Ready for Disaster](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/national/574913/coronial-inquiry-into-whakaari-eruption-hears-country-did-not-seem-ready-for-disaster)
[4] [Emergency Response Time to White Island Eruption Questioned at Coronial Inquest](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/national/576012/emergency-response-time-to-white-island-eruption-questioned-at-coronial-inquest)
[5] [Whakaari Eruption Inquest: Frustrated Families Seek Answers](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/national/575114/whakaari-eruption-inquest-frustrated-families-want-answers)

## Tahi response

Mixed member proportional (MMP) representation was introduced **after** the 1993 election — though the 1993 election played a pivotal role in making it happen.

## The Road to MMP

A binding referendum on whether to change the electoral system was held *at the same time* as the 1993 general election. In that referendum, 53.9% of voters supported adopting MMP, while 46.1% voted to retain the existing first past the post (FPP) system [[1]](cite://1/1). With 85% of eligible voters participating, it was a clear democratic mandate for change [[2]](cite://2/1).

The results of the 1993 referendum meant the Electoral Act 1993 became law, and three years later — on 12 October 1996 — New Zealand held its first general election using MMP [[3]](cite://3/1). So while the decision to adopt MMP was made *at* the 1993 election, the system itself wasn't actually used until 1996 [[4]](cite://4/1)[[5]](cite://5/1).

## How It All Came About

The push for electoral reform had been building for some time. A royal commission set up in 1985 recommended that New Zealand adopt MMP and increase the number of MPs to 120 [[6]](cite://6/1). An indicative referendum in 1992 showed strong public appetite for change, which led the National government to hold the binding 1993 referendum alongside the general election [[1]](cite://1/2).

Under MMP, each voter casts two votes — one for their local electorate candidate and one for a political party — with parliamentary seats allocated in proportion to the party vote [[6]](cite://6/2). The number of MPs increased from 99 to 120 as part of the change [[2]](cite://2/2).

Would you like to know more about how MMP has shaped New Zealand politics in the decades since 1996?

## Tahi response

Helen Clark became prime minister **after** Jenny Shipley.

Jenny Shipley was New Zealand's first woman prime minister, serving from 1997 to 1999. She came to power not through a general election but via a National Party caucus coup, ousting Jim Bolger as leader on 8 December 1997. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)

Helen Clark then followed, serving from 1999 to 2008. She holds the distinction of being the first woman to become prime minister *following an election*, after leading the Labour Party to victory at the polls in 1999. [[3]](cite://3/1)[[2]](cite://2/2)

Together, they were New Zealand's first two women prime ministers — a remarkable pair who put Aotearoa on the world stage as a leader in women's political representation. [[4]](cite://4/1) Jacinda Ardern later became the third, serving from 2017 to 2023. [[3]](cite://3/2)

Would you like to know more about Helen Clark's time as prime minister?

## Tahi response

I'm so sorry to hear about your friend's loss. This is an incredibly difficult time, and it helps to understand what the whānau (family) may be facing in the weeks and months ahead. Here's what the search results tell us about the process in New Zealand following a workplace fatality.

## The Key Regulator: WorkSafe New Zealand

The main workplace health and safety regulator in New Zealand is WorkSafe New Zealand, established in 2013. [[1]](cite://1/1) It investigates workplace deaths under the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015. [[2]](cite://2/1) WorkSafe has a **12-month window** from the time of the incident to investigate and lay charges against any party found to have breached health and safety duties. [[2]](cite://2/2)

When a death occurs, WorkSafe will typically launch an investigation. However, it's important to know that the agency has faced significant criticism in recent years for being slow to prosecute and for taking on only cases it considers likely to succeed — what critics call 'sure thing' cases. [[2]](cite://2/3) A 2024 review commissioned by Crown Law found WorkSafe had a confused approach to prosecutions and that its investigations unit was likely 'seriously under-resourced'. [[3]](cite://3/1)

## The Prosecution Decision

WorkSafe uses an evidential decision-making model to decide whether to prosecute. [[4]](cite://4/1) For a prosecution to proceed, WorkSafe must be satisfied of two things:

- There is a **reasonable prospect of conviction**
- Prosecution is **in the public interest**, in line with the solicitor general's prosecution guidelines [[2]](cite://2/4)

The agency will consult with the family about their views, and those views are taken into account — though they are not the deciding factor. [[2]](cite://2/5) If WorkSafe decides *not* to prosecute, families have limited options for recourse. Real-world cases show that even when WorkSafe's own documentation ticks boxes suggesting prosecution is warranted, it may still decline to act. [[4]](cite://4/2)

## The Coronial Process

Alongside the WorkSafe investigation, there will almost certainly be a **coronial inquiry** — a separate process run by a coroner. It's crucial to understand that the coronial process is *not* a trial and is not about finding fault. Instead, it focuses on determining the facts, identifying causes, and recommending changes to prevent future deaths. [[2]](cite://2/6)

The coronial and WorkSafe processes can interact — for example, WorkSafe may withhold its investigation report until after a coroner's inquiry is complete, to avoid prejudicing it. [[2]](cite://2/7) Families should be aware that this process can take a very long time — in some cases, years. [[5]](cite://5/1)

## A Note on Legal Representation

One significant challenge families face is the **imbalance in legal representation** at coronial inquests. Government agencies and employers often have lawyers, sometimes spending upwards of $100,000 in legal costs, while bereaved families frequently cannot afford legal representation and may not qualify for legal aid (which is restricted to those on low incomes). [[5]](cite://5/2) Experienced lawyers who work in this space have called for reform, arguing families should have access to quality legal support. [[5]](cite://5/3)

## Accident Compensation

New Zealand has a unique no-fault accident compensation scheme administered by the Accident Compensation Corporation (ACC). Under the Accident Compensation Act 2001, rehabilitation support and 80 percent of lost earnings can be paid to those affected by personal injury. Lump sums for pain, suffering, and loss of bodily function are also available. Importantly, New Zealanders cannot sue for damages in relation to personal injury — this was a landmark reform unique to New Zealand. [[6]](cite://6/1)

## The Bigger Picture

Workplace deaths remain a serious issue in Aotearoa. About 70 people die from work-related accidents each year — a higher per capita rate than in Australia or the United Kingdom. [[7]](cite://7/1) The government is currently reviewing the health and safety system, with some shifts in WorkSafe's focus from enforcement toward advice — a change that has alarmed advocates for vulnerable workers. [[8]](cite://8/1)

For your friend's whānau, the road ahead may be long and emotionally exhausting. Knowing they have the right to engage with WorkSafe's process, participate in any coronial inquiry, and seek legal advice (if possible) are important first steps.

Would it be helpful to know more about what families can expect during a WorkSafe investigation specifically?

## Tahi response

## The Invasion of Parihaka — 5 November 1881

On the morning of 5 November 1881, one of the most significant and sobering events in New Zealand history unfolded at Parihaka, a Māori settlement on the slopes of Taranaki. What made it so striking was not the violence of the defenders, but their absolute commitment to peace in the face of overwhelming force.

![embed-1](embed://1)

## Background: A Community Built on Peaceful Resistance

Parihaka had been established in the late 1860s by the prophets Te Whiti o Rongomai and Tohu Kākahi, both of Taranaki and Te Āti Awa descent. [[1]](cite://1/1) It grew to become one of the largest and most prosperous Māori communities in the country, drawing followers from iwi across Aotearoa who were drawn to the prophets' message of non-violent resistance to land confiscation. [[2]](cite://2/1)

The central grievance was the confiscation of vast tracts of Taranaki Māori land following the New Zealand Wars, and the government's failure to set aside the reserves it had promised. [[3]](cite://3/1) In 1878, surveyors began laying out roads and sections on confiscated land, breaking an earlier promise that the area would not be settled until Māori reserves had been defined. [[2]](cite://2/2)

In response, from May 1879, Parihaka men began ploughing settler-occupied land and pulling up survey pegs — a deliberately symbolic act of civil disobedience, using the settler's own farming tools to protest the seizure of Māori land. [[4]](cite://4/1)[[5]](cite://5/1) As each man was arrested and imprisoned without trial, another stepped forward to take his place. [[6]](cite://6/1)

## The March on Parihaka

Native minister John Bryce, determined to destroy what he called 'that headquarters of fanaticism and disaffection', had little trouble recruiting volunteers. [[7]](cite://7/1) Settlers from around the country eagerly signed up, and in camp they trained on Armstrong six-pound field guns. [[7]](cite://7/2)

On 5 November 1881, close to 1,000 volunteers and 500 members of the Armed Constabulary — a force of roughly 1,500 men in total — marched on Parihaka. [[7]](cite://7/3)[[8]](cite://8/1) They arrived to find the 2,500-strong community sitting together, having kept watch through the night. [[9]](cite://9/1) Rather than resistance, the troops were met by singing women who had baked 500 loaves of bread for their 'visitors', and children — known affectionately as *tātarakihi* (singing cicadas) — playing skipping games and singing *waiata* (songs) at the entrance to the pā. [[9]](cite://9/2)[[10]](cite://10/1)

![embed-2](embed://2)

Te Whiti had counselled his people clearly: 'I stand for peace. Though the lions rage, still I am for peace... I am here to be taken.'

## What Happened on the Day

Despite the peaceful reception, the government forces proceeded with their mission. [[11]](cite://11/1) Te Whiti, Tohu, and a third Taranaki prophet, Tītokowaru, were arrested. [[12]](cite://12/1) Around 1,600 outsiders — people who had come to Parihaka from other parts of the country — were expelled and their homes destroyed. [[12]](cite://12/2)

One journalist, who defied Bryce's strict news blackout by entering the pā secretly from the rear, wrote of the scene: 'The whole spectacle was saddening in the extreme; it was an industrious, law-abiding, moral and hospitable community, calmly awaiting the approach of the men sent to rob them of everything dear to them.' [[9]](cite://9/3)

Accounts also record that women were raped, homes ransacked, and crops destroyed. [[13]](cite://13/1) Many of those arrested were transported to Dunedin at the other end of the country and put to work breaking rocks for roads. [[13]](cite://13/2)

## Aftermath

Te Whiti and Tohu were imprisoned without trial and held until 1883. [[14]](cite://14/1) Tītokowaru spent eight months in gaol. [[15]](cite://15/1) In their absence, Parihaka was partially rebuilt, and ploughing campaigns — along with the imprisonment of protesters without trial — continued into the late 1890s. [[14]](cite://14/2)

The invasion of Parihaka is now widely considered part of the broader Taranaki wars, and the day — *te rā o te pahua* (the day of plunder) — is remembered as a profound act of state violence against a community that had chosen peace. [[16]](cite://16/1)[[9]](cite://9/4) Parihaka itself endures as a symbol of non-violent resistance, and in 2006 became the home of the annual Parihaka International Peace Festival. [[17]](cite://17/1)

Would you like to know more about the lives and legacy of Te Whiti o Rongomai and Tohu Kākahi, the prophets at the heart of the Parihaka movement?

## Sources

[2] [Parihaka: The Hidden History of Māori Nonviolent Resistance and Colonial Dispossession in New Zealand](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/parihaka/)
[3] [Arthur Hamilton Gordon: Colonial Governor and the Parihaka Crisis](https://teara.govt.nz/en/biographies/2g13/gordon-arthur-hamilton)

## Cannabis Law Reform in Aotearoa New Zealand: A History

The story of cannabis regulation in New Zealand stretches back well over a century — from a time when it was freely available at the chemist, to prohibition, a failed legalisation referendum, and ongoing calls for reform.

### Before Prohibition: Widely Available and Cheap

Before the 1920s, cannabis was largely unregulated in New Zealand. It was widely available and inexpensive — reportedly costing about a shilling an ounce (roughly $6 for 30 grams in today's money) — and was an ingredient in many patent medicines and pharmacy preparations. [[1]](cite://1/1) Cannabis was even made exempt from customs duty after 1895. [[2]](cite://2/1)

### Early Restriction: International Pressure Shapes Local Law

It was international conventions, rather than any domestic drug crisis, that first brought cannabis under legal control in New Zealand. A 1924 international convention required restrictions on a growing range of drugs, and New Zealand extended those controls to cannabis (then referred to as "Indian hemp") the following year. [[2]](cite://2/2)

The **Dangerous Drugs Act 1927** then formally placed cannabis on a list of dangerous substances. The minister introducing the legislation actually conceded there was no evidence of extensive drug use in New Zealand — the act was designed to bring the country into line with international standards rather than address a local problem. Under the act, cannabis could only be imported or manufactured under licence, and purchased from a doctor or chemist with a prescription. [[3]](cite://3/1)

In 1955, responding to a World Health Organization request, New Zealand agreed to end cannabis imports altogether. [[4]](cite://4/1)

### The 1960s and the Birth of Recreational Use

Until the mid-1960s there was very little recreational use of, or even knowledge about, cannabis in New Zealand. The arrival of 'hippie' culture changed that dramatically, and police responded by establishing vice squads to combat what was seen as an emerging problem. [[5]](cite://5/1) [[6]](cite://6/1)

### The Misuse of Drugs Act 1975

A key legislative milestone came with the **Misuse of Drugs Act (MDA) 1975**, which prohibited the manufacture, import, supply, or possession of controlled drugs and established a three-tier classification system:

- **Class A** — very high risk of harm
- **Class B** — high risk
- **Class C** — moderate risk

Penalties were assigned according to class, and in 1979 deterrent penalties for drug dealing were increased. [[7]](cite://7/1) An Expert Advisory Committee on Drugs was established in 2000 to evaluate substances and recommend classifications. [[7]](cite://7/2)

By 2010, around 9,000 people were being arrested annually in cannabis seizures alone. [[7]](cite://7/3)

### Growing Calls for Reform in the 1990s and 2000s

The 1990s saw increasing public agitation for a more liberal approach to cannabis. The legalisation advocacy group NORML became active, and in 1996 the Aotearoa Legalise Cannabis Party received 1.6% of the vote in the general election. [[8]](cite://8/1) Surveys through the decade consistently ranked cannabis below alcohol, tobacco, hard drugs, and solvent abuse as a community concern, and a 2000 poll found 56% of respondents wanted the law liberalised. [[8]](cite://8/2)

By the early 2000s, New Zealand had one of the highest rates of cannabis use in the world — roughly half of those aged 15–45 had tried it, about one-fifth had used it in the previous year, and around 15% were current users. [[9]](cite://9/1) [[5]](cite://5/2) Cannabis use was notably higher among Māori than the general population. [[10]](cite://10/1)

### Medicinal Cannabis: A 2020 Law Change

Medicinal cannabis became legal and more readily available following a law change in 2020. Products — ranging from oils to dried flower — must meet minimum quality standards and can be prescribed for pain, sleep, and other conditions. Patients must consult a registered doctor who determines whether psychoactive or non-psychoactive products are appropriate. [[11]](cite://11/1)

Subsequent regulatory changes to the **Misuse of Drugs (Medicinal Cannabis) Regulations** have made it easier for New Zealand growers and manufacturers to export, allowing products to meet the standards of importing nations rather than New Zealand's own quality standards. [[12]](cite://12/1)

### The 2020 Recreational Cannabis Referendum

The most significant recent chapter was the 2020 referendum on a proposed **Cannabis Legalisation and Control Bill**. New Zealanders voted against legalisation — a result that some observers attributed to a persistent cultural wariness around hedonism and drug liberalisation. [[13]](cite://13/1) In October 2025, Green Party co-leader Chlöe Swarbrick noted that five years on from the referendum, courts and police resources were still being spent on cannabis prohibition, with 26 people imprisoned for cannabis offences in the previous year alone. [[14]](cite://14/1)

### The Debate Continues

As of late 2025 and into 2026, calls for reform remain active on multiple fronts. The New Zealand Drug Foundation has recommended a complete overhaul of the MDA — calling it a "colossal failure" since its introduction 50 years ago — proposing to scrap the drug classification system and legalise some lower-risk drugs through a not-for-profit model. [[15]](cite://15/1) [[14]](cite://14/2)

The Green Party launched a public consultation platform in October 2025 seeking feedback on what good cannabis regulation might look like, while continuing to advocate for legalisation. [[14]](cite://14/3)

More recently, in April 2026, a member's bill by Green MP Kahurangi Carter — focused on protecting people who call emergency services during a drug overdose from low-level prosecution — passed its first reading in Parliament, reflecting a gradual shift toward viewing drug use as a health issue rather than a criminal one. [[16]](cite://16/1)

The MDA, now over 50 years old, is increasingly viewed by health professionals, researchers, and advocates as outdated — and the debate over what comes next for cannabis law in Aotearoa shows no sign of quieting. [[17]](cite://17/1) [[18]](cite://18/1)

Would you like to explore the specific arguments for and against cannabis legalisation that emerged during the 2020 referendum debate?

# The Foreshore and Seabed Debate: Arguments For and Against

The question of who owns — or has rights over — Aotearoa New Zealand's foreshore and seabed has been one of the most contentious constitutional and Treaty issues of the past three decades. The debate has spanned multiple pieces of legislation, sparked mass protest, and divided political parties. Here's a breakdown of the key arguments on both sides.

## Background

The controversy began in earnest when eight iwi of the upper South Island applied to the Māori Land Court in 1997 to have the foreshore and seabed of the Marlborough Sounds determined as Māori customary land, after failing to be awarded rights for mussel farming. [[1]](cite://1/1) This prompted the Foreshore and Seabed Act 2004, which reserved both the foreshore and seabed for the Crown. [[1]](cite://1/2) That law was later replaced by the Marine and Coastal Area (Takutai Moana) Act 2011, which restored iwi's ability to seek customary rights and title in court. [[1]](cite://1/3) Most recently, the Marine and Coastal Area (Takutai Moana) (Customary Marine Title) Amendment Bill passed its third reading in Parliament in October 2025, tightening the test for customary marine title (CMT). [[2]](cite://2/1)

## Arguments *For* the Legislation (Government's Position)

**Restoring Parliament's original intent**
The government's central argument, advanced by justice minister Paul Goldsmith, was that courts — particularly the Court of Appeal in a 2023 ruling — had interpreted the requirements for CMT in a way that "materially reduced their intended effect." [[2]](cite://2/2) The Supreme Court itself later confirmed the Court of Appeal had taken an "unduly narrow" approach. [[3]](cite://3/1)

**Balancing interests of all New Zealanders**
Goldsmith argued that "everybody in New Zealand has an interest in what goes on in the coastline," and the government was attempting to get that balance right. [[4]](cite://4/1) The 2011 Act was described as recognising Māori rights *while* managing the interests of all New Zealanders. [[5]](cite://5/1)

**Concern over scale of title awards**
Goldsmith cited cases decided since the Supreme Court ruling where "almost 100 percent of the coastline and those areas" were granted CMT, arguing the test still didn't achieve the balance the legislation set out to achieve. [[4]](cite://4/2)

**Parliament's constitutional supremacy**
Law dean Geoff McClay noted that, constitutionally, Parliament *is* supreme and does have the authority to amend legislation when it disagrees with how courts have interpreted it — which is what occurred here. [[2]](cite://2/3)

**Support from Hobson's Pledge**
Hobson's Pledge leader Don Brash supported the bill, saying the courts had reinterpreted Parliament's intent, and that his preference would be to return to the 2004 Act where the Crown owned the foreshore and seabed on behalf of all New Zealanders. [[6]](cite://6/1)

## Arguments *Against* the Legislation

**A form of raupatu (confiscation)**
Critics, including Green MP Hūhana Lyndon and Ngātiwai rangatira Aperahama Edwards, described the amendments as the "biggest raupatu" (confiscation/theft) Māori had faced. [[7]](cite://7/1)[[5]](cite://5/2) Te Pāti Māori MP Tākuta Ferris noted his party was *formed* in direct response to the 2004 Foreshore and Seabed Act and that the 2025 amendments were ripping off even the partial remedy provided in 2011. [[5]](cite://5/3)

**Retrospective application is constitutionally troubling**
Law expert McClay highlighted that retrospectively changing the rules on cases already filed — requiring them to be reheard under a stricter test — breaks a longstanding convention that cases should be decided under the law in place when they were filed. [[2]](cite://2/4) Labour MP Ginny Andersen said the bill "rewrites [Parliament's] intention, narrows it, and applies new rules retrospectively, overturning the work of the courts and reopening wounds." [[2]](cite://2/5)

**Based on a false premise about public access**
Victoria University law lecturer Luke Fitzmaurice Brown argued the bill was "based on the racist myth" that upholding Māori coastal rights would prevent people from accessing public beaches. CMT cannot be sold and has no effect on public access, fishing, or other recreational activities. [[6]](cite://6/2)

**Harmful to Māori-Crown relations**
Former attorney general and Treaty negotiations minister Chris Finlayson — himself a National MP — said the changes would be "extremely harmful" to race relations in New Zealand and did not restore Parliament's original intent. [[5]](cite://5/4)[[8]](cite://8/1)

**'Mean spirited' and punitive**
Veteran Māori lawyer Annette Sykes described the changes as "punitive" and "mean spirited," arguing the retrospective nature was "a breach of good faith" that penalised claimants who had spent years and considerable resources building their cases. [[8]](cite://8/2)

**Waitangi Tribunal opposition**
The Waitangi Tribunal recommended the Crown halt all efforts to amend the Takutai Moana Act, finding failings in the government's handling of the process, including introducing funding changes without consultation and leaving claimants in the lurch. [[9]](cite://9/1)[[10]](cite://10/1)

**Undermining indigenous property rights**
The broader Māori argument rests on the principle that before European law was imposed, Māori practised their own laws and customs which could not be entirely overruled by later legislation — a foundation for all customary title claims. [[11]](cite://11/1)

## Where Things Stand

The Amendment Bill passed its final reading on 22 October 2025 with support from the three coalition government parties. [[12]](cite://12/1) Opposition parties — Labour, the Greens, and Te Pāti Māori — have indicated their opposition, with some promising to repeal the changes. [[13]](cite://13/1) The legislation remains deeply contested, touching on constitutional convention, Treaty obligations, and what it means to share a coastline in a bicultural nation.

Would you like to explore the history of Māori protest movements that have emerged in response to these legislative battles, including the famous 2004 hīkoi?
