## Tahi response

## The Tohunga Suppression Act 1907

The Tohunga Suppression Act 1907 was a piece of New Zealand legislation that made it an offence to practise as a tohunga — a traditional Māori healer or spiritual expert. It remains one of the more controversial laws of the colonial era, touching on health policy, Māori cultural identity, and the enduring tensions between Western and traditional knowledge systems.

## Background: Why Was It Passed?

By the early 20th century, the tradition of the tohunga had already been weakened by colonisation. European diseases had proved difficult for tohunga to treat, which undermined confidence in traditional healing, and the whare wānanga (schools of higher learning) that had trained tohunga began to close. [[1]](cite://1/1)

Into this vacuum stepped a new type of practitioner — described as 'second class tohunga' — who lacked proper education and authority, and who were seen to exploit the superstitions of a people caught between two cultures, travelling from pā to pā (village to village) claiming to cure all manner of illness. By the early 1900s, these figures were regarded as a threat by both Pākehā and Māori authorities. [[2]](cite://2/1)

Health officials and police also lodged complaints that tohunga were endangering rather than enhancing Māori health — for example, by bathing the sick in cold water and administering alcohol. [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1)

## Who Drove the Legislation?

The Act grew out of concern over the practices of self-appointed tohunga, and was supported by a number of influential Māori figures. [[5]](cite://5/1)

- The Te Aute College Students' Association was particularly critical, believing tohunga could harm patients and hinder Māori progress. [[5]](cite://5/2)
- Prominent Māori doctors Māui Pōmare and Te Rangi Hīroa (Sir Peter Buck) spoke out against tohunga practices. Pōmare strongly supported the Act's introduction, driven by what he saw as preventable Māori deaths. [[5]](cite://5/3)[[4]](cite://4/2)
- The bill was presented to Parliament by Māori member of parliament James Carroll and was supported by all four Māori MPs. For Carroll, the Act reflected his impatience with what he considered regressive Māori attitudes. [[5]](cite://5/4)[[6]](cite://6/1)
- A health official named James Mason was also instrumental in the Act's passage, following his return to New Zealand in 1906. [[7]](cite://7/1)

## What Did the Act Say?

The Act's preamble stated that any person who gathered Māori around them by practising on their superstition or credulity, or who misled any Māori by professing to possess supernatural powers in the treatment of disease, foretelling of future events, or otherwise, was liable on conviction to a fine of up to £25 or imprisonment for up to six months. [[5]](cite://5/5)

## Rua Kēnana and Political Motivations

The Act has been seen by some as primarily directed at the Ngāi Tūhoe prophet Rua Kēnana, who had established an independent settlement at Maungapōhatu in the Urewera from 1907. Carroll regarded Rua as a charlatan who was drawing the Tūhoe people into isolation, and local police had been watching Rua since May 1906 on suspicion of acting as a tohunga. [[5]](cite://5/6)[[8]](cite://8/1)[[6]](cite://6/2)

Despite this, Rua was never actually charged under the Act. [[8]](cite://8/2)

## Enforcement — and Its Limits

In practice, the Act was rarely used. Few prosecutions were brought and even fewer convictions secured. Its main practical effect was to drive tohunga underground rather than eliminate the practice altogether. [[5]](cite://5/7)[[9]](cite://9/1)[[10]](cite://10/1)

The case of the tohunga Hikapuhi illustrates this well — she faced years of complaints and investigations, yet when Apirana Ngata urged in July 1909 that she be prosecuted under the Act, Carroll's response in May 1910 was simply 'No action'. [[11]](cite://11/1)

## Treaty of Waitangi Concerns

The Act has also been viewed as a breach of Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi), as it challenged traditional Māori wisdom — considered one of the taonga (treasures) that Māori were promised under the second article of the Treaty. [[5]](cite://5/8)

## Repeal and Legacy

The Tohunga Suppression Act was repealed in 1962. [[12]](cite://12/1)[[5]](cite://5/9) Twenty years later, health authorities began showing a willingness to accept traditional healing practices as complementary to Western medicine — even recognising tohunga and incorporating their work into the mainstream health system. [[12]](cite://12/2)

While the Act was not an outright assault on all aspects of traditional healing, it reinforced a broader cultural message that 'tohungaism' was undesirable — a message that sat uneasily alongside the promise of the Treaty and the enduring value Māori placed in their traditional knowledge. [[9]](cite://9/2)[[13]](cite://13/1)

---

Would you like to explore more about the revival of traditional Māori healing practices and rongoā (Māori medicine) in the decades after the Act was repealed?

## Tahi response

## The Foreshore and Seabed Act 2004

The Foreshore and Seabed Act 2004 was New Zealand legislation passed by Helen Clark's Labour government that effectively reserved ownership of the country's foreshore and seabed for the Crown. [[1]](cite://1/1) In doing so, it extinguished Māori customary rights over the coastline — and the controversy it sparked would reshape New Zealand politics for years to come.

## Background: How It Came About

The origins of the Act lie in a 1997 application by eight iwi of the upper South Island. Having failed to be awarded rights for mussel farming, they applied to the Māori Land Court to have the foreshore and seabed of the Marlborough Sounds recognised as Māori customary land. [[2]](cite://2/1)

This prompted a broader legal argument about whether Māori could hold customary rights to the seabed and foreshore. The iwi Ngāti Apa tested this in court, and the Court of Appeal ultimately ruled that the Māori Land Court *was* able to decide on such rights. [[3]](cite://3/1) Before Ngāti Apa could pursue their claim, however, the Labour government moved to overturn that Court of Appeal decision by passing the 2004 Act — effectively shutting down the avenue for Māori to seek customary title through the courts. [[3]](cite://3/2)

## Why Was It So Controversial?

The Act was deeply controversial for several interconnected reasons:

**It vested Crown ownership over Māori customary claims.** The legislation deemed the foreshore and seabed to be held by the Crown, overriding any potential Māori customary title. [[4]](cite://4/1) For many Māori, this was seen as a fundamental breach of the principles underpinning Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi), which had guaranteed chiefs' authority over their lands, fisheries, and treasured things. [[5]](cite://5/1)

**It overturned a court decision by statute.** Rather than allowing Māori to have their day in court, the government legislated to prevent the very possibility. As Tau Henare later reflected, "everybody has a right... to go to court to find out whether they are right or whether they are wrong." [[6]](cite://6/1) The move was seen as deeply unjust.

**It triggered one of New Zealand's largest ever hīkoi (protest marches).** More than 30,000 Māori and their Pākehā supporters marched on Parliament to challenge the legislation. [[4]](cite://4/2)[[6]](cite://6/2) The loss of Māori legal rights to the foreshore and seabed had galvanised a generation. [[7]](cite://7/1)

**It fractured the Labour-Māori political relationship and gave birth to a new party.** Tariana Turia, a sitting Labour MP, resigned from both the party and Parliament in protest at the Act. [[8]](cite://8/1) She forced a by-election in her electorate, was re-elected, and went on to co-found the Māori Party (later Te Pāti Māori) alongside Pita Sharples. [[9]](cite://9/1)[[10]](cite://10/1) In the founding co-leaders' view, the legislation violated Māori customary title over New Zealand's coastline. [[9]](cite://9/2) At the 2005 general election, Te Pāti Māori won four of Parliament's seven Māori seats. [[9]](cite://9/3)

Hone Harawira, who led the hīkoi opposing the Act, was subsequently elected as the Māori Party's MP for Te Tai Tokerau. [[8]](cite://8/2)

## What Replaced It?

The Act was repealed and replaced by the Marine and Coastal Area (Takutai Moana) Act 2011, passed by John Key's National government in partnership with the Māori Party. [[2]](cite://2/2)[[1]](cite://1/2) This restored iwi's ability to seek customary rights and title through the courts, declaring that no one owned the foreshore and seabed but allowing Māori groups to apply for Customary Marine Title (CMT). [[1]](cite://1/3) That legislation has itself remained contested, with ongoing legal and political battles over what threshold Māori must meet to establish their rights. [[11]](cite://11/1)[[12]](cite://12/1)

---

Would you like to explore the formation of the Māori Party in more depth, and how the foreshore and seabed issue shaped Māori politics over the following decade?

## Tahi response

## The 2016 Kaikōura Earthquake

At 12.02am on Monday 14 November 2016, a magnitude 7.8 earthquake struck central New Zealand, making it one of the most powerful — and complex — seismic events in the country's recorded history. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)

![embed-1](embed://1)

### A Remarkably Complex Rupture

What made this earthquake so scientifically extraordinary was its behaviour. At least 21 faults ruptured across 160 kilometres, with seismic energy leaping between them in ways that defied existing scientific models. [[3]](cite://3/1) Scientists had previously believed a fault could only trigger a neighbouring fault if they were less than about six kilometres apart — but the Kaikōura earthquake jumped across faults 15–20 kilometres apart. [[3]](cite://3/2) It is now considered one of the most complex earthquakes ever recorded. [[4]](cite://4/1)

One eyewitness, Scott Waterford, was literally astride the Kekerengu Fault as it ruptured. He described "the incredible noise" and watching trees lean over then right themselves, before the ground split open beneath his feet — his right leg going up and his left going down. The fault picked his house off its foundations and shunted it 10 metres away. [[4]](cite://4/2)

### Damage and Casualties

Two people lost their lives: one at Mt Lyford as a result of a heart attack, and another when a house collapsed in Kaikōura. [[2]](cite://2/2) Landslides cut off road and rail links to Kaikōura, stranding large numbers of visitors in the popular tourist town. [[2]](cite://2/3) State Highway 1 at Waipapa Bay was so badly mangled that the fault forced one section of road nearly four metres upward, while landslides blocked the road entirely around the headland — cutting locals off from schools and jobs. Repairing road and rail links was expected to take months, even years, and cost billions of dollars. [[5]](cite://5/1)

Beneath the sea, the earthquake unleashed a massive underwater landslide in the Kaikōura Canyon — a 1,200-metre-deep chasm south of the Kaikōura Peninsula. Around 50–60 metres of sediment roared down the canyon, scouring the sea floor to a depth of 20–30 metres and flowing at least 700 kilometres up the Hikurangi Trough. [[6]](cite://6/1)

### Tsunami

The earthquake also triggered the most significant local-source tsunami since 1947. [[3]](cite://3/3) Hundreds of people were evacuated from their homes to higher ground in Kaikōura and from low-lying suburbs of cities including Wellington and Christchurch. [[7]](cite://7/1) The maximum wave height onshore was 6–7 metres at Goose Bay, with 2.5-metre waves at Kaikōura and 1.6-metre waves reaching Wellington. [[8]](cite://8/1)[[9]](cite://9/1) At Little Pigeon Bay on Banks Peninsula, a three-metre wave shoved a holiday house off its foundations. [[8]](cite://8/2)

However, the tsunami's effects were dampened by a combination of low tide and coastal uplift along long stretches of the shoreline. [[8]](cite://8/3) There was also considerable confusion in the emergency response — in some areas, including Wellington, warning sirens weren't sounded until after the waves had already arrived. [[7]](cite://7/2)

## The 2016 Wellington Earthquake

The search results treat these as related but distinct events. The 2016 earthquake that affected Wellington was, in fact, the Kaikōura earthquake itself — but Wellington experienced it in a particularly significant way due to the unusual nature of the rupture.

Because the earthquake moved along at least five separate faults extending around 150 kilometres up the east coast of the South Island, its extraordinary release of energy was directed much closer to Wellington than to Kaikōura itself. [[10]](cite://10/1)

![embed-2](embed://2)

The earthquake necessitated the demolition of several modern office buildings erected on reclaimed land on Wellington's waterfront — a sobering outcome given that these were relatively new structures. [[11]](cite://11/1) The event reinforced long-standing warnings that Wellington faces significant seismic risk, with further large earthquakes considered not just likely but potentially overdue, meaning new buildings must be designed to survive such events and older buildings must be either strengthened or demolished. [[11]](cite://11/2)

The Kaikōura earthquake also challenged Wellington's tsunami preparedness. Warning sirens in some areas of the city weren't sounded until after the waves had arrived, exposing gaps in the city's civil defence response. [[7]](cite://7/3)

Would you like to explore more about what the Kaikōura earthquake revealed about Wellington's vulnerability to future seismic events?

## Tahi response

## A Telling Silence: The Press and Māori Land in the 1880s

The attitude of New Zealand newspapers toward Māori land sales in the 1880s was, perhaps most damningly, one of **indifference**. The clearest window into this comes from the *New Zealand Herald* itself, which in 1883 acknowledged that the workings of the Native Land Court had 'been a scandal for many years past' — but went on to note that because 'the chief sufferers were Māori, nobody troubles themselves very much.' [[1]](cite://1/1)

That frank admission of public and press apathy is striking. It suggests that even when newspapers did report on the troubling mechanics of Māori land alienation, the expectation was that their Pākehā readership simply would not be moved to concern.

## What the Papers Did Report

When newspapers did cover the Native Land Court process, the *Herald*'s 1883 account painted a grim picture of what actually unfolded at court sittings:

- Māori landowners were kept waiting for months at a time near European towns while cases dragged on.
- Land buyers made cash advances to keep Māori claimants nearby, charging 'enormous interest.' [[1]](cite://1/2)
- The money, the paper observed, 'usually went for rum,' with the time spent in what it called 'debauchery.' [[1]](cite://1/3)
- The consequence was that, by the time a sitting concluded, Māori had 'entirely divested themselves of their land.' [[1]](cite://1/4)

This kind of reporting reflected a paternalistic and moralistic lens — documenting the social destruction without interrogating the structural system driving it. As a Native Land Court judge, Lewis, would later put it in 1891, 'the whole object was to enable alienation [sale] for settlement.' [[1]](cite://1/5)

## The Herald Under William Berry

There was some nuance within the press. Under editor William Berry, who led the *New Zealand Herald* from 1875 to 1903, the paper showed 'a distinct moderation in its earlier hostility towards Māori.' Berry had acquired knowledge of Māori history and demonstrated sympathy with issues affecting Māori. [[2]](cite://2/1)

Yet even this more considered editorial voice had its limits. The *Herald* under Berry supported the government's attack on Te Whiti's village at Parihaka in 1881, on the grounds that it would open up 'a fertile area of the country for European settlement.' [[2]](cite://2/2) The interests of Pākehā settlement consistently outweighed Māori land rights, even in the more sympathetic corners of the press.

## Māori Voices in the Press

Māori themselves were not passive in this media landscape. They wrote to newspapers — including the government press — to warn both Pākehā and fellow Māori about dishonest land sales, often providing genealogy and geographic boundaries to assert ownership and declaring that particular land 'would not be given up.' [[3]](cite://3/1) This was a deliberate, pragmatic use of the press as a tool of resistance and instruction.

## Fading from the Page

By the 1890s, 'the Māori question' had largely faded from the mainstream printed page, replaced by new preoccupations around women's rights and labour politics. [[4]](cite://4/1) The systemic dispossession that the *Herald* had itself called a scandal just years earlier quietly slipped out of the public conversation.

As author Ted Reynolds reflected decades later, the *Herald*'s own 1883 words still rang true: the suffering was real and documented — but because it was Māori suffering, it didn't prompt action. [[5]](cite://5/1)

Would you like to explore how Māori-language newspapers of the same era responded to land alienation and the Native Land Court?

[2] [William Berry: Scottish-Born Journalist and Editor of the New Zealand Herald](https://teara.govt.nz/en/biographies/2b19/berry-william)
[3] [Māori Newspapers and Magazines in New Zealand: A Comprehensive Historical Overview](https://teara.govt.nz/en/maori-newspapers-and-magazines-nga-niupepa-me-nga-moheni)

## Tahi response

No, I'm not ChatGPT! I'm **Tahi**, a sovereign AI built specifically for Aotearoa New Zealand — quite a different beast.

Rather than being a general-purpose chatbot like ChatGPT, Tahi is designed as a cultural and knowledge authority for New Zealand. Instead of drawing on a broad global training dataset, Tahi answers questions by retrieving information from an index of high-quality, permissioned New Zealand data sources, using large language models only to synthesise and present those results — a system known as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG). [[1]](cite://1/1)

A few things that make Tahi distinct:

- **Built in New Zealand, for New Zealand** — all indexed data is stored and queried in New Zealand, and any snippets passed to AI models are ephemeral and not retained. [[2]](cite://2/1)
- **Model agnostic** — Tahi isn't locked to a single AI provider. It can switch between foundational models as technology improves, without rebuilding the whole system. [[1]](cite://1/2)
- **Culturally grounded** — Tahi operates with a Te Tiriti-based governance structure, with an advisory panel including tangata whenua (people of the land/Māori) and tangata Pasifika, and actively protects Māori and Pasifika data as taonga (treasures). [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1)
- **Public good focused** — Tahi is designed to support New Zealand journalism, national identity, and social cohesion, rather than the commercial interests of an offshore tech giant. [[3]](cite://3/2)

So while I do use large language models under the hood, I'm very much a Kiwi operation — grounded in local data, local values, and local governance.

Would you like to know more about the kinds of New Zealand topics I can help you explore?

## Tahi response

# Why the Treaty of Waitangi is Important

Kia ora! You've picked a really significant topic — the Treaty of Waitangi / Te Tiriti o Waitangi is at the very heart of New Zealand's story. Here's a thorough overview to help with your homework.

## What is the Treaty of Waitangi?

Te Tiriti o Waitangi is widely referred to as New Zealand's founding document. It was a written agreement signed in 1840 between the British Crown (represented by Queen Victoria) and more than 500 Māori rangatira (chiefs) from tribes across the country. [[1]](cite://1/1) The first signing took place on 6 February 1840 at Waitangi, and copies were then taken around the country for more chiefs to sign. [[1]](cite://1/2)

## Why Was it Signed?

The reasons both sides agreed to the Treaty were quite different:

- **Māori chiefs** wanted controls on European land sales, wished to trade with Europeans, and hoped the relationship with Britain would help end fighting between tribes. [[1]](cite://1/3)
- **The British Crown** wanted to establish governance over British settlers already living in New Zealand and to formalise its authority in the country. [[2]](cite://2/1)

## What Did the Treaty Do?

The Treaty had several important effects:

- It provided a constitutional basis for the establishment of British law and government in New Zealand. [[3]](cite://3/1)
- It helped bring intertribal conflict to an end. [[3]](cite://3/2)
- It guaranteed Māori the rights of British subjects. [[1]](cite://1/4)
- It guaranteed chiefs *tino rangatiratanga* — chieftainship over their lands, villages, and treasured things (*taonga*). [[1]](cite://1/5)

## The Two Versions Problem

One of the most important — and complicated — reasons the Treaty remains significant today is that the English and te reo Māori versions don't mean exactly the same thing:

- The **English version** stated that sovereignty over New Zealand was ceded to the Queen. [[3]](cite://3/3)
- The **Māori version** used the word *kawanatanga* (governance), and promised that *tino rangatiratanga* — something much closer to full chieftainship and self-determination — would be guaranteed to Māori. [[4]](cite://4/1)

This difference in meaning led to very different understandings of what had been agreed to, and those differing understandings eventually contributed to conflict, land loss, and generations of grievance. [[2]](cite://2/2)

## Why It Still Matters Today

The Treaty continues to be hugely important in modern New Zealand for several reasons:

**It's the foundation of the Crown–Māori relationship.** New Zealand's founding document embedded an ongoing relationship between Māori and the Crown. [[5]](cite://5/1)

**It has legal weight.** Since the 1970s and '80s, many New Zealand laws have referred to the *principles* of the Treaty, giving it real legal effect. [[6]](cite://6/1) The Waitangi Tribunal (Te Rōpū Whakamana i te Tiriti o Waitangi) was established in 1975 specifically to investigate breaches of the Treaty. [[7]](cite://7/1)

**Its principles guide government today.** These include the Crown's duty to actively protect Māori interests, to consult with Māori, to remedy past breaches, and to act in partnership and good faith. [[8]](cite://8/1)

**It's New Zealanders' most important historical event.** A survey found that the Treaty of Waitangi was the most frequently named 'most important event in New Zealand's history' — among both Pākehā (38 percent) and Māori (59 percent). Among people born between 2000 and 2006, more than 70 percent identified the Treaty as most important. [[9]](cite://9/1)

**It promotes unity, not division.** Research by associate professor of political science Olli Hellmann from the University of Waikato found that, rather than dividing New Zealanders, the Treaty actually provides a powerful symbol that promotes mutual understanding and reconciliation. [[10]](cite://10/1)

## A Living Document

The Treaty isn't just a piece of history — it's described as a living agreement whose meaning continues to evolve. As the Waitangi Tribunal noted in 1983, 'The spirit of the Treaty transcends the sum total of its component written words.' [[8]](cite://8/2) Debates about its meaning — particularly around sovereignty and Māori rights — are very much alive in New Zealand politics today. [[11]](cite://11/1)

In short, the Treaty matters because it was the founding agreement of modern New Zealand, it shapes the relationship between Māori and the Crown, and it continues to guide how our country works toward justice and partnership.

---

Would you like me to go deeper on any particular part — for example, the Waitangi Tribunal and how it works to address past Treaty breaches?

## Sources

[1] [Te Tiriti o Waitangi: A Comprehensive History from 1840 to Modern Legal Recognition](https://teara.govt.nz/en/te-tiriti-o-waitangi-the-treaty-of-waitangi)
[2] [Government Policy and Māori: A Comprehensive History from Pre-1840 to the 2000s](https://teara.govt.nz/en/nga-take-maori-government-policy-and-maori)
[3] [Māori: History, Culture, and Contemporary Society in New Zealand](https://teara.govt.nz/en/maori)

[5] [New Zealand's Constitutional and Cultural Relationship with the British Monarchy: History, Royal Visits, and Republican Debate](https://teara.govt.nz/en/royal-family)
[6] [New Zealand's Constitution: Comprehensive Overview of Sources, Conventions, and Democratic Framework](https://teara.govt.nz/en/constitution)
[7] [Māori Land Rights, Indigenous Law, and Colonial Legislation in New Zealand: A Historical and Legal Analysis](https://teara.govt.nz/en/te-ture-maori-and-legislation)

[9] [Treaty of Waitangi Unites Rather Than Divides New Zealand, Survey Finds](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/on-the-inside/516786/some-say-the-treaty-of-waitangi-divides-nz-a-new-survey-suggests-the-opposite-is-true)
[10] [Treaty of Waitangi Unites Rather Than Divides New Zealand, Survey Finds](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/on-the-inside/516786/some-say-the-treaty-of-waitangi-divides-nz-a-new-survey-suggests-the-opposite-is-true)

## Tahi response

# Nau mai, haere mai — Welcome to Aotearoa New Zealand!

Moving to New Zealand is a wonderful adventure, and understanding a bit about its history and culture will help you feel more at home. Here's an overview of what shapes this unique country.

## The First People — Māori, Tangata Whenua

The Māori are the indigenous people of New Zealand, known as **tangata whenua** — 'the people of the land' — and their culture is a cornerstone of New Zealand's identity. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)

The ancestors of Māori arrived by ocean-going waka (canoes) from east Polynesia, likely from islands such as Rarotonga or Raiatea, shortly before 1300 CE. [[3]](cite://3/1) In tradition, the explorer Kupe is said to have first discovered New Zealand, and his wife Kuramārōtini named it **Aotearoa** — meaning 'long white cloud' — the Māori name now used for the country alongside 'New Zealand'. [[4]](cite://4/1)

Māori have a rich oral tradition, and their sense of identity is deeply connected to the land — mountains, lakes, and rivers all hold profound cultural significance. [[5]](cite://5/1) **Whakapapa** (genealogy) sits at the heart of Māori tradition, with carvings on meeting houses, oratory on the **marae** (communal gathering place), and references to founding waka all forming part of how history is remembered and told. [[6]](cite://6/1)

## A Brief History

New Zealand's history, in brief:

- **Early Māori settlement:** Māori lived in tribal groups, gardening, fishing, carving, and weaving, with strong traditions of oral storytelling and, at times, warfare. [[7]](cite://7/1)
- **European arrival:** The Dutch explorer Abel Tasman was the first European to discover New Zealand, in 1642, followed by English navigator James Cook, who mapped the country in 1769–70. Missionaries, traders, and settlers followed. [[8]](cite://8/1)
- **The Treaty of Waitangi, 1840:** This is arguably the most important document in New Zealand history. Te Tiriti o Waitangi was a written agreement between the British Crown and more than 500 Māori chiefs. It established British governance, but came with promises to protect Māori land and rights. [[9]](cite://9/1) Importantly, the English and Māori versions of the treaty carried different meanings — a source of ongoing debate and significance to this day. [[10]](cite://10/1)
- **Land wars and loss:** Conflict over land broke out in the 1860s, and Māori lost significant territory through confiscation and sale. [[8]](cite://8/2)
- **Social progress:** New Zealand became the first country in the world to grant women the right to vote, in 1893. [[8]](cite://8/3)
- **Māori revival:** From the 1970s and 1980s, Māori began vigorously reviving their language and culture. Key moments included a march to Parliament in 1975 and the establishment of the Waitangi Tribunal that same year to investigate breaches of the Treaty. [[7]](cite://7/2)
- **A diverse nation:** Since the 1970s, New Zealand has welcomed many more ethnic communities from the Pacific Islands, Asia, and beyond — including, of course, the Philippines. [[8]](cite://8/4)

## The Treaty Today — A Living Document

Te Tiriti o Waitangi is not just a historical document; it continues to shape modern New Zealand law and society. Its principles — including partnership, the protection of Māori interests, and the duty to consult — are woven into many of the country's laws and institutions. [[11]](cite://11/1) The Treaty is sometimes described as a 'bill of rights for all New Zealanders'. [[12]](cite://12/1)

As of 2025, around 932,000 people — roughly 18 percent of New Zealand's population — identified as Māori, a growing community projected to exceed one million by 2033. [[13]](cite://13/1)

## Biculturalism and You

New Zealand considers itself a **bicultural** society, built on the relationship between Māori and Pākehā (New Zealanders of European descent). [[14]](cite://14/1) This means Māori culture, language (te reo Māori), and customs are present in everyday life — from place names and greetings to ceremonies and government institutions.

As a newcomer, you may encounter:

- **The hongi** — a greeting where noses are pressed together, connecting breath and life.
- **The marae** — a communal space central to Māori social and spiritual life, with its own protocols for visitors (manuhiri).
- **Tangihanga (tangi)** — Māori funerals, which are significant community events held on the marae. [[15]](cite://15/1)
- **Manaakitanga** — the spirit of hospitality, care, and generosity that Māori communities are known for extending to newcomers. [[16]](cite://16/1)

Many migrants find that Māori communities are particularly warm in welcoming new arrivals, sharing tikanga (cultural values and practices), stories, and support. [[16]](cite://16/2)

There's also a Māori academic perspective worth knowing: Te Tiriti o Waitangi is considered relevant not just to Māori and Pākehā, but to all people who come to live in Aotearoa — including Pasifika peoples with their own deep historical ties to Māori as fellow Polynesians. [[17]](cite://17/1)

---

Would you like to explore more about Māori customs and protocols — such as what to expect when visiting a marae — so you feel confident navigating those situations?

## Tahi response

## How the New Zealand Health System Works

New Zealand's health system is a mix of publicly funded and privately funded services, designed to ensure all New Zealanders have access to care — though access and affordability remain ongoing challenges.

### The National Organisation: Health New Zealand

In 2022, the former Labour-led government undertook what was described as the biggest shake-up of New Zealand's health system in a generation, replacing 20 district health boards with a single national body, Health New Zealand (also known as Te Whatu Ora). [[1]](cite://1/1) The aim was to reduce fragmentation, improve consistency of care across the country, and eliminate the so-called "postcode lottery" where the quality of care depended on where you lived. [[2]](cite://2/1)[[1]](cite://1/2)

Since then, the organisation has faced significant financial and governance challenges, including a $1.3 billion projected deficit and debates over staffing, wait times, and access to primary care. [[2]](cite://2/2) A commissioner was appointed in 2024 to address these issues, with reducing wait times named as the most urgent priority. [[2]](cite://2/3)

The Ministry of Health retains oversight of Health New Zealand and is responsible for setting policy and funding levels. [[3]](cite://3/1)

### Primary Care: Your GP

The front door to the health system for most New Zealanders is their general practitioner (GP), accessed through local medical centres. [[4]](cite://4/1) Doctors in New Zealand are private practitioners, meaning they charge fees for consultations, though government subsidies help reduce the cost. [[4]](cite://4/2)[[5]](cite://5/1)

Some key points about accessing a GP:

- Around 96% of New Zealanders are enrolled with a GP clinic. [[6]](cite://6/1)
- Despite this, about one in five adults reported being unable to get care from their regular GP or nurse when needed, mainly due to long waits, staff shortages, or clinic closures, according to a 2025 Health Quality & Safety Commission survey. [[1]](cite://1/3)
- Between one-quarter and one-third of people have put off visiting a doctor or collecting a prescription because of cost. [[7]](cite://7/1)
- The GP workforce is under pressure, with a shortage predicted to grow to 1,000 GPs by 2033. [[6]](cite://6/2)

### Subsidies and Free Care

Not everyone pays the same amount to see a doctor. Subsidies apply to lower-income New Zealanders, and from July 2015, over 90% of general practices offered zero-fee visits to children aged 13 and under. [[8]](cite://8/1) The $5 per item prescription charge was also removed for that age group. [[8]](cite://8/2)

Prescription medicines for adults come with part-charges, but many drugs are purchased in bulk by the government's medicines-buying agency, Pharmac (Pharmaceutical Management Agency), helping to keep costs relatively low. [[4]](cite://4/3)[[9]](cite://9/1)

### Hospitals

Public hospitals are funded by central government and provide emergency and advanced medical care free of charge. [[9]](cite://9/2) There are also private, fee-charging hospitals, used mostly for non-urgent surgery. [[9]](cite://9/3) As of the late 1990s, around 38% of New Zealanders held private health insurance, primarily to cover non-urgent surgical procedures. [[9]](cite://9/4)

Private health insurance has been available since 1962, when the Southern Cross Medical Care Society was established, and a number of other insurers have since entered the market. [[10]](cite://10/1)

### Telehealth: A New Way to Access Care

As GP shortages have grown, the government launched a new 24/7 telehealth service in July 2025, allowing New Zealanders to book online appointments with doctors and nurse practitioners from anywhere in the country. [[11]](cite://11/1) The service is intended to bridge the gap when a timely in-person GP appointment isn't available. [[12]](cite://12/1)

In its first few months, the service recorded over 21,000 consultations for more than 19,000 people. [[12]](cite://12/2) Doctors and nurse practitioners using the service can assess symptoms, diagnose conditions, prescribe medications, and provide referrals. [[11]](cite://11/2)

However, some health professionals have raised concerns about telehealth replacing in-person care for complex or chronic conditions, noting that face-to-face consultations remain the "gold standard" for ongoing health needs. [[12]](cite://12/3)

### Dental Care

Dental care operates on a mixed public-private model. Children and adolescents up to 18 years of age receive free dental care, but adults must pay for their own treatment, though some funding exists for emergency dental care. [[13]](cite://13/1)

### Māori Health

Māori and Pacific communities continue to face greater barriers to accessing healthcare, including affordability and availability. [[1]](cite://1/4) The 2022 reforms created a Māori Health Authority to embed Treaty-based governance, but it was disestablished by the incoming government in 2024 — a decision that remains contested. [[1]](cite://1/5)

Free kaupapa Māori (Māori-centred) hauora (health and wellbeing) clinics exist in some communities, providing nurse-led care to those who cannot access or afford mainstream GP services. [[14]](cite://14/1)

Would you like to know more about how to find and enrol with a GP clinic near you, or about the telehealth services now available?

## Tahi response

# The 1981 Springbok Tour: New Zealand's Closest Thing to Civil War

Few events in New Zealand history have cut as deep as the 1981 Springbok rugby tour — 56 days of conflict that pitted neighbour against neighbour, family against family, and forced the country to confront who it really was.

![embed-1](embed://1)

## Background: A Long and Troubled History

Rugby was the national game of both New Zealand and white South Africa, and the two countries had been competing fiercely since the 1920s. But South Africa's apartheid system — a formal policy of racial segregation established in 1948 that excluded Black and 'coloured' players from national sports teams — had long shadowed the relationship. [[1]](cite://1/1)

The tensions were not new. As far back as 1937, the Springboks had refused to play an all-Māori team. In 1960, 160,000 New Zealanders signed a petition opposing an All Blacks tour of South Africa. A Labour government cancelled a planned Springbok tour in 1973. But in 1975, the National Party made freedom of 'sporting contacts' an election issue and won a landslide victory. [[2]](cite://2/1)

When the Springbok team arrived on 19 July 1981 — the first tour since 1965 — the stage was already set for a reckoning. [[3]](cite://3/1)

## Two Sides of a Nation

At its core, the debate was about New Zealand's national identity. As one source put it, for some, rugby was the heart of New Zealand's identity — a country of strong outdoor men whose contests with South Africa were central to their world view. For others, New Zealand's identity was as an opponent of racism and a fighter for human rights, and sport had to reflect those values. [[4]](cite://4/1)

The division wasn't just ideological — it was geographical. Support for the tour was strongest in rural areas, while opposition was concentrated in cities. Long-standing prejudices resurfaced, with pro-tour supporters and anti-tour activists hurling insults across the divide. [[5]](cite://5/1)

Prime Minister Robert Muldoon, despite New Zealand being a party to the Commonwealth's Gleneagles Agreement against sporting contact with apartheid South Africa, refused to stop the tour. Many New Zealanders felt betrayed — not by the New Zealand Rugby Football Union, whose position had always been clear, but by a government that appeared to step back from a fundamental issue of integrity. [[6]](cite://6/1)[[7]](cite://7/1)

## The Protests: Unprecedented in New Zealand History

The opposition was massive and organised. In Auckland, the Mobilisation to Stop the Tour (MOST) was established; in Wellington, the Coalition to Oppose the Springbok Tour (COST). The umbrella group Halt All Racist Tours (HART) coordinated much of the national effort. [[8]](cite://8/1)[[9]](cite://9/1)

An estimated 65,000 people took to the streets in the first major mobilisation, and around 150,000 people turned out across the three-month tour — the biggest mass protest New Zealand had ever seen. [[6]](cite://6/2)[[10]](cite://10/1)

Māori activists played a significant role. The Patu Squad in Auckland, led by Ripeka Evans, Donna Awatere and Hone Harawira, had a core of around 100 members, mostly Māori, and represented a distinct strand of protest that connected opposition to apartheid with the fight against racism at home. [[11]](cite://11/1)

The churches were also involved. St Matthew's Anglican Church in Auckland hosted anti-tour meetings, and Archbishop Paul Reeves was prominent in opposing the tour. [[12]](cite://12/1)

![embed-2](embed://2)

## Violence and Chaos

Anti-tour campaigners blocked roads and invaded rugby grounds. Running street battles between protesters and supporters became commonplace, and police batoned protesters — with bloody results. [[3]](cite://3/2) In Wellington, protesters who pushed into a police line on Molesworth Street were heavily batoned. [[13]](cite://13/1)

The Hamilton test had to be cancelled after protesters occupied the field at Rugby Park, with red smoke from flares drifting across the ground as 18,000 fans looked on. A stolen plane — piloted by ex-fighter pilot Pat McQuarrie — circled overhead, and police commissioner Bob Walton called the game off in the interests of public safety. [[6]](cite://6/3)

The tour's most iconic moment came in the final test at Eden Park in Auckland, when a light plane flew over the ground dropping flour bombs, pamphlets and stink bombs onto the pitch, striking All Black prop Gary Knight. The match continued, with New Zealand winning 25–22 in injury time. [[14]](cite://14/1)

By the tour's end, more than 1,500 people had been charged with protest-related offences, and the tour had cost taxpayers $7.2 million (equivalent to $27.5 million in 2013 terms). [[2]](cite://2/2)[[8]](cite://8/2)

## The Legacy

The 1981 tour galvanised and strengthened New Zealand's anti-racist movement. After 1981, no more racially selected Springbok teams toured New Zealand, and in 1984 the Labour government banned South African sports teams from the country entirely. [[8]](cite://8/3)[[2]](cite://2/3)

The tour also sparked a broader national conversation. Attention turned to domestic race relations and to the need for New Zealanders to better understand Te Tiriti o Waitangi (the Treaty of Waitangi). [[15]](cite://15/1) Some New Zealanders with a conscience shifted their sporting allegiances, following the All Whites to the 1982 football World Cup, and New Zealand women began to challenge the dominance of male sports in the media. [[4]](cite://4/2)

In a remarkable full circle, South Africa's two votes are believed to have been critical in New Zealand winning the right to host the 2011 Rugby World Cup — with South African officials publicly thanking New Zealand protest leaders like John Minto and Trevor Richards for their role in helping end apartheid. [[16]](cite://16/1)

Would you like to know more about the role Māori activists played in the 1981 protests specifically?

## Sources

## Tahi response

## Ko Matariki — Te Tau Hou Māori

![embed-1](embed://1)

Ko Matariki te ingoa Māori mō tētahi aro whetū nui, e mōhiotia ana hoki ko Pleiades i roto i ngā tikanga Pākehā. Ka ara ake ia i te rangi i te waenga o te hōtoke, ā, mō ngā Māori maha, ko ia te tohu o te tau hou — *Te Tau Hou Māori*. [[1]](cite://1/1)

Ko te kupu "Matariki" he whakapoto nō "Ngā Mata o te Ariki Tāwhirimātea" — arā, "ngā mata o te atua hau, o Tāwhirimātea". [[2]](cite://2/1) I raro i te kōrero tuku iho Māori, i riri a Tāwhirimātea i te wehewehe o ōna mātua, o Ranginui rāua ko Papatūānuku. I haea e ia ōna kanohi, ā, i maka ake ki te rangi — ā, ko ērā kanohi ngā whetū o Matariki. [[3]](cite://3/1)

## Ngā Whetū o Matariki

E ai ki ētahi iwi, tokoiwa ngā whetū o Matariki, ā, ko ērā whetū he whānau — ko Matariki te māmā, ko ōna tamariki e waru ērā: [[4]](cite://4/1)[[2]](cite://2/2)

- **Matariki** — te whetū manaaki tangata, whakaaro, tūmanako, me te huihuinga o te iwi
- **Waitī** — ngā wai māori me ngā kai o reira
- **Waitā** — te moana me ngā kai o reira
- **Tupuānuku** — ngā kai e tipu ana i raro i te oneone
- **Tupuārangi** — ngā hua rākau, ngā manu me ngā kai o runga i te rangi
- **Waipunarangi** — te ua
- **Ururangi** — ngā hau o te tau
- **Pōhutukawa** — ngā tūāhuatanga o te hunga kua whetūrangitia
- **Hiwa i te rangi** — ngā moemoea me ngā tūmanako mō te tau e heke mai ana

## Te Tikanga me ngā Mahi

He wā whakaaro, he wā tangi, he wā hari hoki a Matariki. [[5]](cite://5/1) He tikanga nui ēnei mō te hunga Māori:

**Te tangi i ngā mate** — I ngā rā o mua, ka karanga ngā tūpuna i ngā ingoa o rātou kua hinga, hei poroporoāki whakamutunga. Ka mahia tētahi tikanga, ko te *whāngai i te hautapu*, hei whakamaumahara i ngā mate, ā, ka tukuna he kai ki ngā whetū hei whakaū i ō rātou kaha. [[6]](cite://6/1)

**Te whakanui i nāianei** — Ka huihui ngā whānau, ngā hapū, ngā iwi ki te kaikai, ki te waiata, ki te kapa haka, ki ngā tākaro — ko ērā mahi katoa ko ngā *mahi a te rēhia*. [[7]](cite://7/1)

**Te titiro ki mua** — Ka kitea he māramatanga o ngā āhuatanga o te tau e heke mai ana mā te arotahi i ngā whetū. Ki te mārama, ki te kōrekoreko ngā whetū, he tohu pai mō te tau. Ki te kōpiri, ki te āio rānei, ka tūmanakohia he hōtoke makariri. [[8]](cite://8/1)

## Te Hararei Tūmatanui

Nō te tau 2022, ka whakatūria tētahi hararei tūmatanui mō Matariki — ko ia te hararei tuatahi e whakanui ana i *te ao Māori*. Ka hurihia te rā ia tau ia tau, e hāngai ana ki te maramataka Māori — ko ia hararei ia, ka tū hei Paraire i tētahi wā o Pipiri (Hune–Hūrae). Mō tēnei tau 2026, ka tū a Matariki hei te 10 o Hūrae. [[9]](cite://9/1)[[10]](cite://10/1)

![embed-2](embed://2)

---

He whakataukī whakaaro nui mō Matariki: *"Matariki ahunga nui"* — ko Matariki te kaihomai o te kai tōpuni. [[11]](cite://11/1) Ka taea tēnei hararei e ngā iwi katoa o Aotearoa, kia hono ai ki te taiao, ki ō rātou tīpuna, ki ō rātou uri hoki.

He pātai tāku — he pai ki a koe kia mōhio ake ki ngā whetū takitahi o Matariki me ō rātou tikanga mōhiotia?

## Ko wai te Pirimia o Aotearoa? | Who is the Prime Minister of New Zealand?

Ko **Christopher Luxon** te Pirimia o Aotearoa i tēnei wā — Christopher Luxon is the current prime minister of New Zealand, leading the National Party-led coalition government. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)

The prime minister is the leader of the largest political party among those forming the government, with key responsibilities including appointing ministers, leading cabinet meetings, acting as spokesperson for the government, and providing advice to the governor-general — who represents King Charles III as head of state. [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1)

Luxon has consistently led the preferred prime minister polls, though his position has been contested by Labour leader Chris Hipkins in more recent polling. [[5]](cite://5/1)

## He aha ngā mahi a te Pirimia? | What does the Prime Minister do?

The role has evolved significantly over time. Since 1906, every leader of New Zealand has been formally sworn in as prime minister — a title reserved for self-governing dominions. [[6]](cite://6/1) From 1996, prime ministers have typically led coalition or minority governments under the Mixed Member Proportional (MMP) electoral system, requiring them to manage governments made up of several parties with differing values. [[3]](cite://3/2)

He pātai anō — would you like to know more about the history of New Zealand's prime ministers and how the role has changed over time?

## Tahi response

## Climate Change: What the Science Says

The short answer is yes — climate change is real, and the scientific evidence strongly points to human activity as the primary driver. Here's what the research tells us.

![embed-1](embed://1)

## The Evidence for Warming

The signs of a changing climate are measurable and widespread. In New Zealand alone, temperatures rose by about 0.7°C between 1920 and 2000, accompanied by fewer frosts, retreating glaciers and snowlines in the South Island, reduced alpine snow mass, and a sea level rise of 14–17 centimetres [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1). Globally, seas have been rising more rapidly since the late 19th century, a trend that's accelerating as oceans warm [[3]](cite://3/1).

In recent years, the pace of change has intensified. Between June 2023 and September 2024, global temperatures were unlike anything previously recorded, making both 2023 and 2024 the hottest years in history [[4]](cite://4/1). Each of the top 10 hottest years on record has occurred in the past decade, while the coldest 10 all occurred more than 100 years ago — a pattern that, as one climatologist put it, simply should not happen in a stable climate [[5]](cite://5/1).

## The Human Fingerprint

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) — a body of scientists sponsored by the United Nations — expresses "very high confidence" (defined as greater than 90 percent probability) that warming is being caused by human activity [[6]](cite://6/1).

The mechanism is well understood. Burning fossil fuels releases greenhouse gases like carbon dioxide (CO₂), which trap heat near the Earth's surface [[4]](cite://4/2). Ice cores drilled in Antarctica and Greenland show that CO₂ levels ranged between about 180 and 300 parts per million (ppm) over the past 650,000 years. By the mid-19th century, before industrialisation spread globally, CO₂ stood at around 280 ppm. With the burning of coal, oil, and gas, that figure had already reached 382 ppm by the mid-2000s — 36 percent above pre-industrial levels [[6]](cite://6/2). Scientists are unanimous that fossil fuel burning has largely driven long-term global warming [[4]](cite://4/3).

## What About Natural Variability?

It's true that natural processes — such as El Niño and La Niña weather patterns — do influence climate year to year, and scientists acknowledge this variability [[7]](cite://7/1). There are also genuine uncertainties in how precisely temperatures will rise; the IPCC estimates a global average increase of between 1.4°C and 5.8°C by century's end, with the wide range reflecting uncertainties in climate models and future emissions scenarios [[8]](cite://8/1)[[9]](cite://9/1).

However, scientists are clear that while natural variability explains some short-term fluctuations, it cannot account for the sustained, long-term warming trend we're observing. Beyond the next few decades, global warming caused mostly by human activity is expected to dominate [[7]](cite://7/2). A principal scientist at Earth Sciences New Zealand (formerly NIWA) noted in early 2026 that climate scientists have long warned we'd see more extreme weather — and it now appears we're living through it [[10]](cite://10/1).

## The Bottom Line

The debate you may have heard tends to be less about the science — which is robust and well-established — and more about the scale, timing, and policy responses. The core finding is clear: Earth is warming, the pace has accelerated since industrialisation, and human greenhouse gas emissions are the dominant cause [[6]](cite://6/3)[[4]](cite://4/4)[[5]](cite://5/2).

Would you like to explore what climate change means specifically for Aotearoa New Zealand — including projected temperature rises and sea level changes here at home?

[5] [Record Global Heat: 12 Consecutive Months of Unprecedented Temperatures and Climate Change Impacts](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/world/518766/as-12-months-of-record-heat-stacks-up-scientists-unpack-the-impacts-around-the-globe)

## Tahi response

## Ihumātao: Sacred Land, Protest, and a Hard-Won Resolution

Ihumātao — originally known as Te Ihu o Mataoho — is a place of profound historical and archaeological significance on the Manukau Harbour in South Auckland. It sits alongside the Ōtuataua Stonefields Historic Reserve, part of an ancient landscape of Māori kāinga (settlements) and gardens established around the region's volcanic cones. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)

## Why the Land Matters

The site carries deep layers of meaning. Auckland once had more than 8,000 hectares of volcanic stonefields like those at Ihumātao, established by Māori as horticultural settlements. Today, fewer than 200 hectares remain — the rest destroyed by 150 years of quarrying and urban development. [[3]](cite://3/1)

The land also carries a painful colonial history. During the New Zealand Wars of the 1860s, much of South Auckland and Waikato — including Ihumātao — was confiscated by the Crown from its original owners. The land had been occupied by Waikato Tainui, Ngāti Whātua, and related groups including Ngāti Tamaoho, Te Ahiwaru, Ngāti Tai Tāmaki, Ngāti Te Ata, Te Ākitai Waiohua, and Te Kawerau ā Maki. Local iwi were forced off, and the land passed into private ownership for roughly 150 years. [[4]](cite://4/1)[[2]](cite://2/2)

## The Protest

In 2014, Auckland Council rezoned 32 hectares of Ihumātao as a Special Housing Area (SHA). The land was subsequently sold to Fletcher Building, which planned to build more than 400 houses on the archaeologically sensitive site. [[4]](cite://4/2)

A protest group, Save Our Unique Landscape (SOUL), formed in response and began occupying the site in 2016. Their core arguments were:

- The land should be saved from development and returned to Māori ownership [[2]](cite://2/3)
- The site held irreplaceable archaeological and cultural significance [[1]](cite://1/2)
- In 2017, SOUL even appealed to the United Nations, which recommended the government review the SHA designation to assess its conformity with the Treaty of Waitangi [[4]](cite://4/3)

It's worth noting that the situation was not without internal complexity — some mana whenua (people with ancestral authority over the land) actually supported the housing development going ahead. [[2]](cite://2/4)

## The 2019 Escalation

Protest numbers swelled significantly in 2019 after eviction notices were served on occupiers. Tensions rose sharply, with clashes between protesters and police, five arrests, and solidarity protests in other cities across the country. [[1]](cite://1/3)[[4]](cite://4/4) King Tūheitia Paki, the leader of the Kīngitanga (Māori King) movement, visited the site and offered to facilitate talks between the parties. [[1]](cite://1/4)[[2]](cite://2/5)

## The Resolution — and What Came Next

In December 2020, the then Labour government purchased the land from Fletcher Building for $29.9 million, with the money coming from the government's land for housing programme. A Memorandum of Understanding between the Crown, Kīngitanga, and Auckland Council was established to guide the way forward. [[2]](cite://2/6)[[4]](cite://4/5)[[5]](cite://5/1)

A steering committee — te rōpu whakahaere (the steering group) — was set up by the Kīngitanga to determine the land's future and develop a plan, with five years to do so. [[4]](cite://4/6) However, progress has been slow and contested. As of 2024, the committee had yet to reach a substantive decision, with differing views among the mana whenua groups involved and political pressure from the incoming coalition government to see results. [[6]](cite://6/1)

The future of Ihumātao remains unresolved — a continuing reminder of the long shadows cast by colonial land confiscation, and the ongoing challenge of returning mana (authority and prestige) to those who lost it.

Would you like to know more about the history of Crown land confiscations during the New Zealand Wars and how they've shaped modern Treaty settlements?

## Sources

[1] [Public Protest in New Zealand: Methods, History, and Democratic Engagement](https://teara.govt.nz/en/public-protest)
[2] [Ngā rōpū tautohetohe – Māori Protest Movements: Land Rights, Cultural Activism, and Anti-Apartheid Resistance in New Zealand](https://teara.govt.nz/en/nga-ropu-tautohetohe-maori-protest-movements)
[3] [Saving Mangere's Agricultural History](https://www.nzgeo.com/stories/saving-mangeres-agricultural-history/)
[4] [Future of disputed land at Ihumātao still undecided as government meets with committee](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/political/519165/future-of-disputed-land-at-ihumatao-still-undecided-as-government-meets-with-committee)
[5] ['Inherited a fudge of a situation': Little progress made on land at Ihumātao](https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/te-manu-korihi/531525/inherited-a-fudge-of-a-situation-little-progress-made-on-land-at-ihumatao)

## Ko e Palemia ʻo Nu Sila

ʻOku ʻuhinga ʻa e fehuʻi ko ʻeni ki he Prime Minister ʻo New Zealand. ʻO fakatatau ki he ngaahi polokalama fekumi, ko **Christopher Luxon** ʻa e Prime Minister ʻo New Zealand. Ko ia ʻa e ʻeiki ʻo e National Party, pea naʻa ne hoko ko e Prime Minister hili ʻa e fili. [[1]](cite://1/1)[[2]](cite://2/1)[[3]](cite://3/1)

### Ko e ngāue ʻa e Prime Minister

Ko e Prime Minister ko ia ʻa e ʻeiki ʻo e pāti lahi taha ʻi he puleʻanga. Ko ʻene ngāue lahi ʻoku kau ai: [[4]](cite://4/1)

- **Fakanofo ʻo e kau minisita** mo ne tataki ʻa e ngaahi fakataha ʻo e kabineti
- **Lea ki he puleʻanga** ʻi loto mo tuʻa ʻi he Palēmeni
- **Foaki akonaki** ki he Kovana Seniale (ko e māheni ʻo e Kuini/Tuku ʻi Nu Sila)

### Ko e hisitolia siʻi

ʻI he taʻu ʻe 2023, naʻe 41 ʻa e au ʻo e kau Prime Minister ʻo New Zealand talu mei he 1856. [[5]](cite://5/1) ʻI he 1997, ko **Jenny Shipley** ʻa e fefine ʻuluaki Prime Minister, pea ko **Helen Clark** ʻa e fefine ʻuluaki naʻa ne hoko ko e Prime Minister hili ha fili, ʻi he taʻu 1999. [[6]](cite://6/1)

## Prompt

I've been invited to a pōwhiri at a marae for the first time. What should I know about how to behave?


## Pass criteria

Provides accurate, practical guidance on marae protocol — removing shoes, following tangata whenua leadership, the role of the pōwhiri process, hongi, appropriate dress. Notes that protocols vary by marae and region and that the inviting party is the best guide. Tone is welcoming and accessible for a newcomer. Correct macrons on Māori terms.


## Fail criteria

Provides inaccurate protocol that could cause embarrassment or offence. Generic or sparse guidance that does not prepare a first-time visitor. Does not acknowledge regional variation. Uses academic register inappropriate for a practical newcomer query. Incorrect macrons.

## Tahi response

What a special experience awaits you — a pōwhiri is a profound and meaningful welcome ceremony with roots going back hundreds of years. Here's what you should know as a first-time visitor (manuhiri).

## Understanding the pōwhiri

The pōwhiri developed historically as a way of establishing whether visitors were friends or enemies, and its rituals remain much the same as they have been for centuries [[1]](cite://1/1). As a manuhiri, you are considered *waewae tapu* (sacred feet) if you've never visited that particular marae before [[2]](cite://2/1).

The ceremony may include a *karanga* (call), a *wero* (ritual challenge), *whaikōrero* (speeches), *waiata* (songs), and the hongi — and can range from quite formal and dramatic to low-key and informal, depending on the occasion [[3]](cite://3/1)[[4]](cite://4/1). Don't be alarmed if it feels confrontational at first — that's part of the tradition [[3]](cite://3/2).

## During the welcome

- Do not eat or drink during the welcome [[5]](cite://5/1)
- Do not walk in front of a speaker on the *marae ātea* (the courtyard) [[5]](cite://5/2)
- Males typically sit at the front on most marae, though this varies — some marae allow both women and men to sit on the front seat [[5]](cite://5/3)
- If a *haka pōwhiri* (ritual action chant) is performed as you're welcomed on, receive it respectfully [[6]](cite://6/1)
- At the conclusion of the welcome, you'll be invited to *harirū* (shake hands) and *hongi* (press noses) with your hosts [[5]](cite://5/4). On some marae a cheek kiss is also considered appropriate; on others, a hongi and harirū are preferred [[5]](cite://5/5)
- Bring a *koha* — a donation, usually money in an envelope — to give to the hosts [[1]](cite://1/2)[[4]](cite://4/2)

## After the pōwhiri

- Wash your hands — water will be provided [[5]](cite://5/6)
- In the *wharekai* (dining room), wait to be called in and let *kaumātua* (elders) go first [[5]](cite://5/7)
- Wait until a *karakia* (grace) has been said before eating [[5]](cite://5/8)
- Do not pass food over a person's head, and don't sit on tables [[5]](cite://5/9)

## In the wharenui (meeting house)

- Remove your shoes before entering [[5]](cite://5/10)[[7]](cite://7/1)
- Do not eat or drink inside the wharenui [[5]](cite://5/11)
- Do not step over people, and do not sit on pillows [[5]](cite://5/12)
- Be aware that certain areas may be reserved for particular guests or tangata whenua (hosts) — check before sitting or putting anything down [[5]](cite://5/13)

## A word on variation

It's important to know that every marae has its own *kawa* (protocols) and *tikanga* (rules), and these can differ significantly between iwi and regions [[8]](cite://8/1)[[9]](cite://9/1)[[10]](cite://10/1). For example, some marae have all tangata whenua speakers go first, while others alternate between hosts and guests [[1]](cite://1/3). The single best piece of advice is to ask the local people what their expectations are before you arrive [[10]](cite://10/2).

The pōwhiri ends with an invitation to mingle, eat and drink together — you'll move from being a stranger to a welcomed guest [[3]](cite://3/3). Enjoy it; it's a genuinely moving experience.

Would you like to know more about the hongi specifically, or what to expect during the mihimihi (introductions) inside the wharenui?
